"Subhūti, foolish persons who think to find fault with this perfection of wisdom and think to reject it will also go forth into homelessness in this very Dharma and Vinaya.

"Subhūti, just as some dog might abandon the food its master gives it and think to seek food from a servant, so in times to come there will be sons of good family and daughters of good family of the bodhisattva vehicle who will abandon this profound perfection of wisdom, which is the root of all the qualities of a buddha, and think to rely on sūtras associated with the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, which are like branches, leaves and petals. Subhūti, a great bodhisattva should recognize that as the work of Māra.

"Subhūti, moreover, in times to come there will be those of the bodhisattva vehicle who, for the sake of gain and honor, will think to master the sūtras associated with the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. That too is the work of Māra.

"Likewise, if someone who has found an elephant thinks to go looking for its footprints, or if someone who wishes to see the great ocean thinks to go looking for a cow's hoofprint, and on seeing it wonders, 'Is this equal to the ocean or not?', is that one wise?" Subhūti replied: "He is not."

Attention associated with the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas also arises as the work of Māra. Likewise, just as those are unwise who, wishing to build a mansion like Vaijayanta, take its measurements from the celestial palace of the sun and moon; who, wishing to see a universal monarch, see a petty king of some province and treat him as his equal; who reject food of a hundred flavors and go looking for coarse sixty-day rice; and who, having found a priceless jewel, think to compare it with a cheap stone; so too the wish associated with the Lesser Vehicle, which rejects the perfection of wisdom, arises as the work of Māra.

It should be understood that if those of the bodhisattva vehicle are not upheld by the mind of omniscience, they will stray aside midway. "Subhūti, what is it that they stray aside into? It is the ground of the śrāvakas or the ground of the pratyekabuddhas."

He said: "Subhūti, the tathāgatas should be known as the spiritual friends of the great bodhisattvas. The great bodhisattvas are spiritual friends. Those śrāvakas who speak of and teach the six perfections, who explain and comment on them, who analyze and clarify them, and who correctly show them, should also be known as spiritual friends of the great bodhisattvas."

He asked: "Blessed One, what is the characteristic of the perfection of wisdom?" "Subhūti, the perfection of wisdom has the characteristic of non-attachment, like space. The perfection of wisdom is without characteristic; the perfection of wisdom has no characteristic whatsoever."

He asked: "Is there a sense in which, by the very characteristic through which the perfection of wisdom exists, all phenomena exist?" He said: "Subhūti, so it is, so it is. By the very characteristic through which the perfection of wisdom exists, all phenomena exist. Why is that? Subhūti, all phenomena are isolated by nature: all phenomena are isolated from all phenomena, and all phenomena are empty of all phenomena. Subhūti, in this sense, by the characteristic of emptiness and the characteristic of isolation, the characteristic through which the perfection of wisdom exists, all phenomena exist."

He said: "Nanda, this perfection of wisdom extensively teaches all the dharmas in which those of the śrāvaka vehicle, those of the pratyekabuddha vehicle and those of the bodhisattva vehicle are to train, and by training in which, just as taught, each comes to abide on his own ground. Nanda, this profound perfection of wisdom is the entry into all letters."

He said: "Subhūti, although the linking on by habitual tendencies is not itself an affliction, the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas still show bodily manners and manners of speech that belong to attachment, aversion and delusion. Their linking on by habitual tendencies does not do harm as it does for all childish ordinary beings; and in the Tathāgata it is absent in every way and is not apprehended."

He asked: "Why is it called the perfection of wisdom?" He said: "Subhūti, this perfection of wisdom has reached the supreme far shore of all phenomena; therefore it is called the perfection of wisdom."

"All these phenomena are dependently originated, born from inversion, and held by the maturation of karma. Why then do you perceive all phenomena, which are not real things, as real things?" So, when the great bodhisattva is practicing the perfection of wisdom, with skill in means he turns miserly beings away from miserliness and joins them to the perfection of giving; and their giving things away also becomes a cause of great wealth.

The meditative absorption of emptiness is one-pointedness of mind in the aspect of emptiness and the aspect of isolation. Signlessness is one-pointedness of mind in the aspect of peace and the aspect of signlessness. Wishlessness is one-pointedness of mind in the aspect of impermanence and the aspect of suffering.

How is dependent origination known? To know dependent origination as without arising, without cessation, without annihilation, without permanence, not one in meaning, not different in meaning, without coming, without going, free from conceptual elaboration, and at peace: knowing it in this way is knowing it.

"Subhūti, what do you think? Does an arhat engage in either grasping or not grasping a dream-cognition?" He replied: "Though there are arhats, they do not sleep; how then could they engage in grasping or not grasping a dream-cognition?"

He said: "Subhūti, all phenomena are emanations. Some are emanated by śrāvakas, some are emanated by pratyekabuddhas, some are emanated by bodhisattvas, some are emanated by tathāgatas, some are emanated by afflictions, and some are emanated by karma."

"Those foolish persons will reject the perfection of wisdom for four reasons." He asked: "What are the four?" He said: "The foolish persons who reject this profound perfection of wisdom are under the sway of Māra. Subhūti, for that first reason they will reject this profound perfection of wisdom. They have no trust in profound dharmas, and no faith or devotion. For that second reason they will reject this profound perfection of wisdom.

"Subhūti, those foolish persons have fallen into the hands of evil friends; they are without diligence and cling to the five aggregates. For that third reason they will reject this profound perfection of wisdom. They are of hateful conduct, praising themselves and disparaging others. For that fourth reason they will reject this profound perfection of wisdom." So it says.

In the Perfection of Wisdom in Seven Hundred Lines it says: "Mañjuśrī, a son of good family or daughter of good family who wishes to enter the meditative absorption of the single array should take up residence in solitude and delight in freedom from busyness. Without attending to any signs, he should sit cross-legged, and there, by way of non-apprehension, attend to a single tathāgata. He should also utter the name of the tathāgata to whom he attends, and having heard that name and focused on it, he should sit facing the direction in which that tathāgata dwells. If he attends to that one tathāgata, he will have attended to all the tathāgatas of the three times. Why is that? Because tathāgatahood is one. Just as the buddha qualities of a single tathāgata are immeasurable and his eloquence is immeasurable, so it should be understood that one who has attained the meditative absorption of the single array has arising in him, from the one non-arising, immeasurable distinctions of the categories of the Dharma."

"Kāśyapa, you should understand that those who in times to come hear this profound perfection of wisdom, which is unborn and unarisen and so on up to without real existence, and are glad at heart and rejoice, are seated in this very assembly of mine."

Mañjuśrī said: "Blessed One, not abiding in anything whatsoever is itself meditating on the perfection of wisdom. Moreover, Blessed One, not thinking about and not considering any phenomenon whatsoever should be regarded as meditating on the perfection of wisdom."

In the Perfection of Wisdom Requested by Suvikrāntavikrāmin it says: "Suvikrāntavikrāmin, all phenomena arise from inversion, and so they are like a mirage. Suvikrāntavikrāmin, what is called 'union' is permanence; what is called 'separation' is annihilation. Suvikrāntavikrāmin, all phenomena arise from inversion, and in that inversion there is neither union nor separation. Why is that? Suvikrāntavikrāmin, the thing that is inversion is not to be apprehended, and its arising, too, is not apprehended. Suvikrāntavikrāmin, inversion is nonexistent; it is unreal, false, deceptive and hollow by nature. Since no phenomenon whatsoever is apprehended in it, it is called inversion. What is called inversion deceives beings. It is beings' imagining of what is unreal."

"Śāriputra, these phenomena do not exist in the way that childish ordinary beings conceive of them and so cling to them." He asked: "Blessed One, in what way do these phenomena exist?" The Blessed One said: "They exist in the way that they do not exist. Since they do not exist in that way, they are therefore called nonexistent." He asked: "Why is that which is not nonexistent called existent?" He said: "Because of the eighteen emptinesses, form is called not existent." The same is to be applied to the rest.

Then the elder Subhūti thought: "These flowers that the gods have scattered I have never before seen to exist, even in all the abodes of the gods; these flowers are emanations. These flowers scattered by the sons of the gods did not grow from shrubs, did not grow from water, and did not grow from the ground. These flowers have arisen from the mind; these flowers did not arise from shrubs."

Kauśika asked: "Blessed One, what are these two that are the nature of reality?" They are the nature of reality of all conditioned phenomena and the nature of reality of unconditioned phenomena. "What, then, is the nature of reality of conditioned phenomena?" It is everything from the gnosis of inner emptiness up to the gnosis of worldly phenomena and the gnosis of transworldly phenomena. "What, then, is the nature of reality of unconditioned phenomena?" It is that in which there is no arising, no ceasing, no abiding, no change while abiding, no affliction, no purification, no increase and no decrease. That is the essential nature of all phenomena.

"If even a son of good family or daughter of good family who has entered the bodhisattva vehicle and who, in a dream, up to the time of sitting at the seat of awakening, meditates on the perfection of wisdom, enters into the equipoise of meditative concentration, undertakes diligence, practices patience, guards moral discipline and gives gifts, is to be understood as drawing near to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, then, Blessed One, what need is there to speak of a great bodhisattva who actually draws near to it by meditating on the perfection of wisdom and the rest?"

In the Dense Array it says:

The domain of the bodies of embodied beings
Abides in Ghana, yet is not in Ghana.
That field is stainless merit,
Ghana, the essence, supremely beautiful.
Likewise, in liberation and the knowledge of liberation,
It is the supreme field among fields,
The excellent field of the buddhas;
That Ghana should be regarded as a domain.

With those ten masteries,
The powers, the superknowledges and the meditative absorptions,
And with a body arisen from the cause that is the mind,
That buddha is resplendent.
Ghana is supreme liberation and wisdom;
The sphere of phenomena and miraculous power, too,
Are dense, the abode of yogins.
Having passed far beyond the net of darkness,
The field of Ghana is resplendent.
This region is accomplished by the word;
It is not the domain of those bound to causes.

When particles are broken down,
The entity of a particle does not remain;
Since their nature is supreme above all,
They are therefore called supreme particles.

The eight qualities of mastery,
Subtlety and the rest, the quality of greatness,
Attainment, the fulfilment of wishes, renown,
Lordship, possession of control,
And being able to do whatever one wishes:
Having gained these, in the central land of Ghana
They dwell and joyfully play.

So too the ground consciousness,
Ever possessed of habitual tendencies,
Though it keeps company with the seven kinds of consciousness,
Does not mingle with them.
The blazing splendor of gods and asuras
And all paths and lineages are under its sway;
The beauty of the fields of all the buddhas, too,
Has come about with it as their cause.

When life, warmth and this consciousness
Have abandoned this body,
Then this body is cast away
Like a log, without feeling.
That is the ground consciousness;
The mind is held to be the warmth;
Mental consciousness dwells in it,
And the rest dwell in the five faculties.

Those who teach the Dharma
Without a trustworthy scripture,
Through conceiving of characteristics,
Ruin themselves and ruin others.

The world is not an accumulation of particles;
It does not go into directions and places.
Those particles do not build up anything
By twos or by threes.
As for those who dwell from abode to abode in the realms of the gods,
It is understood likewise on the various grounds.

When they have discriminated and seen
The aggregates and the person as selfless,
But have not wholly destroyed phenomena as real things,
Those śrāvakas attain clear realization.

In the King of Meditative Absorptions, the recollection of the Buddha appears, beginning, "Thus that Blessed One is ..."

Just as the Teacher, the Sugata, has explained,
Know the distinctive sense of the sūtras of definitive meaning.
Wherever beings, persons and men are taught,
Know all those teachings to be of provisional meaning.

Though worldly people meditate on meditative absorption,
It does not destroy their notion of a self.
Their afflictions arise again and again,
As when Udraka meditated on meditative absorption here.

The wise do not quarrel with the childish;
They avoid the childish while showing them respect.
Thinking, "He comes to me with a hateful mind,"
The childish never truly rely on the Dharma.
Knowing the nature and disposition of the childish,
The wise do not keep company with the childish;
For however well one attends on the childish,
They turn back and become like enemies.

The harmony and union of the childish with the childish
Is like filth with filth;
The harmony and union of the wise with wise people
Is like butter with its cream.
When one shows the childish helpful affection,
The childish rejoice if harm befalls him.
Because of such faults, abandon the childish,
And dwell alone in the wilderness like a deer.

The wise, knowing faults such as these,
Think, "If I rely on those of lesser wisdom, I shall fall from the higher realms;
How then could I attain awakening?"
And so they never rely on the childish at all.

Unsurpassed giver of fearlessness, homage to you!
Benefactor of others, loving kinsman of beings, homage to you!
Possessor of the ten powers and of true prowess, homage to you!
Sugata, equal to the unequalled, homage to you!
Homage to the protector, supremely compassionate!
Homage to the hero who has subdued the four foes!
Homage to you who know the true meaning in every dispute!
Homage to the god who possesses the supreme Dharma body!

Sun of the Victors, with the light of compassion and love, dispeller of darkness,
Risen in the center of the sky of vast wisdom,
Teacher of the most profound dharmas of ultimate truth:
To you, O god, I too go for refuge always.

All these beings will become buddhas;
Here there is no being who is not a vessel.
The qualities of the Sugata are immeasurable.
Supreme among humans, gone to the far shore of qualities,
Arrived at the far shore of all distinctive qualities,
Buddha, field of merit, I bow my head to you.

Candraprabha, the youthful, knowing that the Blessed One was seated with his retinue, presented great offerings one after another, and served and offered him food.

Thus those bodhisattvas
Who have wholly abandoned habitual tendencies
Are not carried away by forms;
They abide in the family of the buddhas.

He said:

This very Candraprabha, the youthful,
In the past, here in the city of Rājagṛha,
Saw ten billion buddhas.

The body has the characteristic of awakening;
The buddhafield too is likewise;
The powers, the liberations and the meditative concentrations, too:
All of them have a single characteristic.

Thus will buddhas appear;
Such protectors of the world
No one can ever see
With fleshly eyes.

The great hero is the Dharma body;
His body has arisen from the Dharma.
By way of the form body
A buddha can never be designated.

Though people, having seen the body of a buddha,
Say, "I have seen the Guide,"
No one is able to behold me
As a form body.

Whatever errors one may see in anyone,
Do not speak of their faults.
Whatever kind of deed one has done,
That kind of fruit one will obtain.

The benefits of devoting oneself to dwelling in the wilderness are ten: one dwells with few activities; one keeps away from gatherings farther than far; one is free of quarrels there; one dwells without harm; one does not increase the defilements; one does not become a ground for disputes; one conducts oneself in utter peace; one dwells well restrained; the mind becomes a stream conducive to liberation; and one swiftly realizes liberation.

"Youth, you should train thus: 'Giving up the happiness of the kingdom of a divine universal monarch, I shall go forth.' And having gone forth, you should abide in the qualities of ascetic purification and in having few possessions, abide in truthfulness, and perfect patience and gentleness."

Long ago, after the Buddha King of Masses of Splendor had taught the Dharma, King Steadfast Gift went forth. Then the people of Jambudvīpa went forth one after another; by the power of the Buddha, rice grew without plowing or sowing, Dharma robes appeared of themselves, and service was rendered by the gods.

In thousands of world systems,
Whatever sūtras I have taught,
Their words are different, their meaning one;
It is impossible to recite them all.
If one meditates on a single word,
All of them will have been meditated on.

However many buddhas there are,
All of them have taught many dharmas.
Because they know the nature of dharmas,
That all dharmas are emptiness,
They are therefore called buddhas;
And they guide inconceivable,
Immeasurable beings to awakening.

Those who know the essential nature
Of dharmas that are like optical illusions
Do not regard the body of a buddha
As a body of form.
This Dharma has no bodily form;
In it there is no bodily form at all.
Where dharmas have no bodily form,
That is the body of a buddha.
Those who look upon the Dharma body
Are the ones who see the Guide.
The perfect Buddha is the Dharma body;
One who sees that sees the perfect Buddha.

They increase saṃsāra,
And those ordinary beings wander on.
Because they go each their separate ways,
They are called "separate folk," ordinary beings.

Whoever has the perception,
"This perception must be abandoned,"
Is engaged in the elaboration of perception;
He is not freed from perception.
Who brings forth these perceptions?
Who has produced this perception?
Who has touched perception?
Who will make perception cease?
The dharma from which perception arises
Not even the buddhas have found.
Reflect here on that meaning,
And so you will become free of perception.

Abiding on the ground of thought,
One should reflect step by step;
When all thought has been abandoned,
Then one goes beyond thought.
This is the ripening of white dharmas;
It is seen through the absence of formations.
What all beings are thinking
He fully knows in a single instant.

All these buddhafields, too,
Are beautifully adorned with jewel trees,
And pools filled with water of the eight qualities
Are emanated there.
Those who dwell on the banks of the pools,
Having drunk water from them,
All have their craving removed,
And become shrines even for the world.

Likewise, lotuses and the like are emanated, and by smelling their fragrance and hearing the sounds that resound from them, qualities arise such as the abandonment of the three poisons.

The bodhisattva Kṣemadatta, as an offering to the Tathāgata Ghoṣadatta, wrapped his right arm in cotton cloth and set it alight as a lamp, and he proclaimed the sūtra of the King of Meditative Absorptions in a voice both loud and gentle. The earth shook, and the gods too, with faith arisen, made offerings. It is told that he then accomplished much for the benefit of beings, such as many of the gods generating the mind set on awakening.

The giving of Dharma and of material things is done in accordance with dedication. One dedicates it thus: that one may obtain that skill in means by which the tathāgatas became fully awakened; that one may meet a spiritual friend who will teach the swift attainment of unsurpassed awakening; that one may meet with resources by which all beings will be sustained; and that one may take on a body through which one will be able to benefit all beings with Dharma and with material things.

When they bear malice toward one another,
Moral discipline and learning cannot protect them;
Meditative concentration does not protect them, dwelling in the wilderness cannot protect them,
Giving does not protect them, nor does worship of the buddhas.

Those who, when the true Dharma of the supreme among men
Is being destroyed, cultivate indifference,
Have not honored the Victors;
They have shown no respect to the Guides.

King Śūradatta killed the monk Supuṣpacandra.

Never train on the ground of the śrāvakas,
And take no delight in their conduct either.
Not letting the mind stray from the qualities of a buddha,
One swiftly becomes a buddha, lord of kings.

Through the unattractive, cut off attachment always;
Through loving-kindness, destroy aversion wholly, without remainder;
Through wisdom, clear away the net of the affliction of delusion;
Having attained meditative absorption, one shines in all the worlds.

What is miraculous power? Accomplishment, aspiration and the complete fulfilment of prosperity: this is called miraculous power.

What is the thorough seeking of learning? It is holding and meditating on all of the śrāvaka piṭaka, the pratyekabuddha piṭaka and the bodhisattva piṭaka. What is regard for ascetic purification? It is relying on one's vow. What is having a sense of shame? It is not speaking idle chatter. What is relying on holy persons? It is relying on buddhas, bodhisattvas, śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. What is avoiding persons who are not holy? It is avoiding those who fixate on objects and those who are lazy. What is the mind's not being dejected even without gain? It is examining what one has done oneself. What is not associating with householders? It is avoiding all material things. What is relying on going forth? It is abandoning what is improper and seeking what is proper. What is the ground of the practice of yoga? It is meditating on the thirty-seven factors of awakening.

The Dharma of the Buddha is vast,
And its way, too, is taught as vast.
Because the dharmas are taught so vastly,
Vast qualities are obtained.
Just as space is vast,
So is the characteristic of dharmas;
It is filled with vast rays of jewel light,
And therefore it is known as "very extensive."
The conduct of beings is vast,
And the teachings given to them are vast too;
The meaning of the scriptures, too, is vast,
And therefore it is known as "very extensive."

In the Questions of the Nāga King Sāgara it says: "Śakra, king of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three; Suyāma of the Yāma heaven; Santuṣita of Tuṣita; Sunirmita of the Heaven of Delight in Emanation; Vaśavartin of the Heaven of Mastery over Others' Emanations; the Caravan Leader, king of the gods of the class of Māra; the Foremost Brahmā of the Ministers of Brahmā; Sahāmpati, lord of the Sahā world, of the Retinue of Brahmā; Ābhāsvara of the Radiant heaven; Śubhakṛtsna of the heaven of Total Virtue; Bṛhatphala of the heaven of Great Fruit; Maheśvara of the Pure Abodes; and Vimala of Akaniṣṭha: they wish to hear the Dharma of the Buddha and do not wish for the dharma of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; they are lovers of the Dharma. They delight in talk of the mind of awakening, not in talk of the Lesser Vehicle. They abide delighting in the pleasure garden of the Dharma."

Because a bodhisattva sees five benefits, he is insatiable in seeking learning. The five are: from learning, wisdom arises; one becomes free of regret and doubt; one comes to understand affliction and purification; one dispels the afflictions of beings; and with fearlessness one cuts through the doubts of beings. And again, for two further reasons: to give rise to right view, and to attain unattached retention.

Seeing five benefits, a bodhisattva becomes inclined to go forth. The five are: the deeds I have done will not be wasted; the deeds I have done are imprinted by habitual tendencies; the mind lets go of all possessiveness; one is not reproached by the buddhas; and having been freed oneself, one will teach the Dharma so as to free others.

When a bodhisattva has fully attained unshakable faith, sees things as arising from inversion, and fully understands all phenomena to be like a dream, he abides in the way of the profound Dharma.

A bodhisattva endowed with two qualities does not fall into the view of annihilation: he knows how to engage in the workings of karma, and he knows how to accomplish all the qualities of a buddha. Endowed with two, he does not fall into the view of permanence: he enters into the impermanence of phenomena, and he knows how to discern that things, once arisen, disintegrate and dissolve and do not remain.

What is the meditative concentration of a bodhisattva who abides in the abode of wisdom? He does not cultivate the limbs of concentration of body and mind, nor intention and application; rather, he enters equipoise in such a way as one enters equipoise through suchness, the limit of reality and the sphere of phenomena. That outshines all the equipoises of those who fixate on objects. His roots of virtue and those meditative concentrations are predominantly wisdom and free of the afflictions of views. Dedication to awakening and the ripening of beings are also accomplished. Therefore the Tathāgata, with a single equipoise, continues right up to his passing into nirvāṇa.

A bodhisattva who comes into an assembly, sits down, and interrupts talk of the profound Dharma by chatting about all sorts of things is striving to make the true Dharma decline. Moreover, people who long for the Dharma and listen to it are few, while those who listen to what is not the Dharma are many. If they abandon the speaker of the Dharma and chat about all sorts of things, the gods there who are devoted to the profound Dharma are displeased. Saying, "They sit here talking worldly talk. Alas, how wretched! The Dharma of the Buddha is not being heard," they utter harsh words and leave that assembly.

King Ajātaśatru said: "Blessed One, the bodhisattvas will purify buddhafields." He said: "So it is. All phenomena arise from attention, and because all phenomena are without an agent, they are accomplished just as one wishes."

King Ajātaśatru said: "Blessed One, when the Blessed One goes to the great ocean, where is that mass of water?" He said: "When a monk has entered equipoise in the meditative absorption of the fire element and sees the great trichiliocosm completely filled with fire, where has the body of water gone then?" He replied: "That comes from a mind that has gained mastery over meditative absorption becoming workable in that way." He said: "If you hold that this happens through the workability of a śrāvaka's mind, do you not hold the same for the Tathāgata, who has mastery over all phenomena? When I, the Tathāgata, do so, the beings who dwell in water think that they are dwelling in water, while those who dwell on dry land see the great ocean as dry."

For the gods, the suffering is the shared lot of dying and passing on;
For humans, the suffering is seeking and strife;
For the hells, the suffering is torment;
For animals, it is devouring one another;
For hungry ghosts, this is the suffering: hunger and thirst.

In the Journey of the Buddha it says: Then King Prasenajit, seated before him, took off the jeweled garment that he held and wore, offered it to the Blessed One, and said: "Blessed One, this jeweled garment of mine has come about through the maturation of past karma; please accept it." He said: "Great king, you should strive in these four things. A king, a kṣatriya consecrated on the crown of the head, should go to śramaṇas and brahmins endowed with moral discipline and ask them questions; should act to protect the teaching of the Tathāgata; should be eager to seek devotion to the profound; and should be eager to become established in rightness by being free of wrong views."

In the Full Embrace of the Roots of Virtue it says: "Son of good family, in brief, all the qualities of a buddha depend on seeking the Dharma. A bodhisattva is called a bodhisattva because his intention is close to the Buddha."

As for what is called awakening: whatever is emptiness, that is awakening. Of what is that which is called emptiness empty? It is empty of all phenomena.

The superior intention of a bodhisattva should be understood in three respects. The first superior intention has as its activity the thorough seeing of all things. The second is seeking, taking up and holding the Dharma without clinging to it as self. The third is not rejecting the profound Dharma, and entering upon the awakening of a buddha without discouragement.

These four are causes of happiness: seeing the Tathāgata; having perfect leisure; finding faith in the Dharma and Vinaya of the Tathāgata; and having perfect, upright view. Examples are the householder Victory Guard, the wife Rūpavatī, and the Śākya son Dark Gift.

One who is endowed with four qualities does not lose mindfulness even after changing lives. The four are: seeking the Dharma and being insatiable in giving it; causing other beings to take up unsurpassed awakening and proclaiming the qualities of the Tathāgata; and being receptive to the profound Dharma. And: being undistracted in order to please the Tathāgata; having mindfulness and vigilance in order to attend to the Dharma; and being receptive to the profound Dharma and making it one's preparation for death.

Four qualities accomplish the supreme entry into the Great Vehicle. The four are: when the awakening of the Buddha is being destroyed and the true Dharma is declining, striving to seek the true Dharma; when the stūpas of the Tathāgata are being destroyed, not giving up the Dharma even when one is later harmed and it comes to body and life; on seeing suffering beings, giving rise to compassion; and, having gained compassion, undertaking diligence and thinking, "When I have become a buddha, I shall teach the Dharma in order to remove the suffering of those beings."

When a bodhisattva benefits beings with the Dharma, he brings about undefiled happiness; but when beings repay him with clothing and the like, that is defiled. How then could they ever repay him?

Śāradvatīputra said: "From this day forward, whenever we teach the Dharma, we shall teach it beginning first of all with the bodhisattva vehicle, and only afterward teach the śrāvaka vehicle. Why is that? If we establish some beings in great awakening, we shall have repaid the kindness of the Tathāgata." He said: "Śāriputra, it is good that you have put on such armor. Why is that? Because there will be those who scorn such a vehicle and revile it, and who reject such sūtras."

One who is endowed with four qualities brings relief to himself and dwells in happiness. The four are: having entered the Great Vehicle, giving away all one's wealth and possessions; abiding in the aggregate of moral discipline; being exalted in wisdom of the profound Dharma and not giving up the Dharma even for the sake of body and life; and supplying a monk who speaks the true Dharma and upholds the true Dharma with every comfort and requisite.

One who is endowed with four qualities does not fall away from great awakening. Even after changing lives he becomes a universal monarch, and his roots of virtue come into effect just as he has aspired. Having attained the rank of universal monarch, he also goes forth and attains the four abodes of Brahmā, and then, after dying and passing on, he is born in the world of Brahmā. The four are: seeing a ruined stūpa of the Tathāgata, being eager to restore it, and building even so much as a single lump of clay into it; building stūpas, that is, in places where many people gather, such as crossroads, building a stūpa or a many-storied hall, or a pillar, or anything whatever that recalls the qualities of the Tathāgata from his renunciation up to his passing into nirvāṇa; on seeing the śrāvaka Saṅgha of the Tathāgata split, quarreling and in dispute, striving to reconcile them so that they are undivided and in harmony; and, when the Dharma of the teaching is being destroyed, striving to protect even so much as a single verse and keep it from declining.

One who is endowed with four qualities obtains, after changing lives, the going forth conferred with the words "Come, monk." The four are: delighting in going forth oneself and causing others, too, to take up going forth; relying on going forth in every way; and, having gone forth, gladdening others with talk connected with the Dharma and causing them to take it up. Again: striving oneself in order to seek the Dharma and causing others, too, to strive; being oneself endowed with patience and gentleness and establishing others in them; oneself having few possessions and being perfectly content, and establishing others in these; and oneself being skilled in means and having the power of aspiration, and establishing others in it.

One who is endowed with four qualities does not fall away from great awakening. The four are: generating the mind set on awakening with superior resolve; delighting in the teaching of the Tathāgata; being insatiable in listening to the Dharma; and speaking the truth by not deceiving.

Those who attend upon monks
Will become greatly learned.
They are honored by the King of Dharma;
They are the ones who follow after me.

How does a bodhisattva fall away from the Dharma? Though he teaches the Dharma of emptiness, he teaches it putting material gain first; and though he teaches those dharmas, he does not himself engage in them accordingly. In that way he falls away from the Dharma.

In the Meeting of the Father and the Son it says: "If the qualities of the Tathāgata were something with form, then the mass of merit and qualities of each single generation of the mind of awakening could not be told even by tathāgatas for as many eons as there are grains of sand in the river Ganges. Why is that? When the Tathāgata was engaged in the conduct of a bodhisattva, there was not a single generation of the mind that did not arise for the benefit and happiness of all beings, however many they may be. Just as the realm of beings is immeasurable and inexhaustible, so great will the merit be. Such a generation of the mind cannot be repaid even by the offerings of all beings. Worldly offerings are material things, but the generation of the mind is free of material things, because it turns beings who are bent toward saṃsāra so that they are bent toward nirvāṇa."

This very Blessed One Śākyamuni, long ago, countless ages in the past, was the Tathāgata named Indraketu.

No phenomena whatsoever exist by their own nature;
Conceptual thought and every arising of phenomena are empty too.
Through the absence of conception, signlessness comes about;
That itself is awakening; the wise do not speak of it otherwise.

"Buddha" is merely a convention;
Likewise the powers and the wisdom,
And likewise the four fearlessnesses, too,
Were declared by the Sage to be conventional.

In the Cloud of Jewels it says: "Thus one who is not yet skilled in the mind examines phenomena, thinking, 'These phenomena are like an illusion; these phenomena are like a dream; these phenomena are virtuous; these phenomena are unvirtuous; these phenomena arise; these phenomena do not arise.' He then thinks: 'All these phenomena abide in the mind, arise from the mind, and have the mind as their condition.' When he has thoroughly mastered, thoroughly tamed and thoroughly understood the mind, all those phenomena too become thoroughly mastered, thoroughly tamed and thoroughly understood. Seeing phenomena in this way, he applies himself earnestly, by that cause and that condition, to pacifying the mind, and he does not fix upon the mind itself."

How is a bodhisattva skilled in the means of dedication? Whatever kinds of flowers or kinds of fruit there are that are claimed as no one's own and possessed by no one, he offers them three times by day and three times by night to the buddhas and bodhisattvas, and he dedicates that root of virtue to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. In the same way he dedicates flowering trees and the like. Hearing of vast kinds of offerings made to the sūtras, he dedicates them from the depth of his heart.

How is a bodhisattva skilled in the means of raising beings from one position to another? When he sees beings who are able and capable of fully awakening to unsurpassed awakening, but who first strive, exert themselves and undertake diligence in the vehicle of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas, he raises them from that position, even if it means separating them from their circle there, and establishes them in the Great Vehicle. In this way he is skilled in the means of raising beings from one position to another.

A bodhisattva does not teach the Dharma to beings who are not vessels: not to those who bear grudges, nor to fault-finders, tīrthikas, the irreverent, those full of faithlessness, those ridden by deceit and pretense, those whose minds are set on their livelihood, those ridden by gain, honor, envy and miserliness, or the foolish, the dull, and those mute as sheep. Why is that? Although he has no miserliness about the Dharma and does not turn his back on the welfare of beings, he remains equanimous, thinking, "These beings have not become vessels for the Dharma and Vinaya of the Tathāgata."

To what kind of beings do the buddhas and bodhisattvas teach the Dharma? They teach it to beings who are faithful, whose roots of virtue have ripened, who have become vessels, who are without deceit, who do not put on airs in their deportment, who are not attached to gain and honor, who are of good character, who are able to explain well, whose faculties are sharp, and who act as they have been told.

A bodhisattva upholds the Great Vehicle; toward those who uphold it he is respectful and reverent, perceiving them as companions in the Dharma; and he is inclined toward the Great Vehicle, flows toward it, and has entered into it. Therefore his mind of awakening is not damaged.

What, then, are the faults? Desiring gain and honor; attachment to family, clan and views; not regarding small misdeeds with fear; not being afraid of death; thinking about what should not be thought about; perceiving what is not the path as the path; attachment to the affairs of the moment; speaking in dispraise of the Great Vehicle and of the persons who teach it; speaking in praise of the śrāvaka vehicle and of the persons who teach it; devotion to quarrels; being rough, harsh, very brazen and abusive; using unpleasant words; chattering idly; speaking without restraint; and mere talk. Being thus skilled with regard to faults, in order to be free of all elaboration he makes meditation on emptiness his yoga. This is the discipline of the bodhisattvas.

In the Descent into Laṅkā it says: "Lord of Laṅkā, just as, though they arise from a single seed, there are the different forms of sprout, shoot, stalk, joint, branch, flower, fruit and awn, so too, in all the phenomena that come forth from ignorance, the inner and outer phenomena that are born, the aggregates, elements, sense sources and so forth, and in all the phenomena born in the three realms, there appear differences of pleasure, of shape, of speech and of movement. There appear, too, differences of superior, middling and inferior in the way consciousness, which has a single characteristic, engages in grasping at objects, and things appear as purified, as virtuous and as unvirtuous. Lord of Laṅkā, it is not only in their distinctive differences: when the characteristic of individual self-knowing appears even on the path of yogins who cultivate yoga, how could there fail to be a difference between dharma arising from individual conceptual thought and non-dharma? There is."

"Mahāmati, if a lump of clay were something different from its atoms, it would not be made of them; but since it is made of them, it is not different. Yet if it were not different, there would be no distinction. Likewise, if the evolving consciousnesses were different from the characteristic of the genus of the ground consciousness, they would not arise from it. If they were not different, then when the evolving consciousnesses ceased, the ground consciousness too would cease; but its own genus-characteristic does not cease. Therefore the own genus-characteristic of the consciousnesses does not cease; it is the characteristic of activity that ceases. If the own genus-characteristic ceased, the ground consciousness too would cease; and Mahāmati, if the ground consciousness ceased, this teaching would be no different from the tīrthikas' teaching of annihilation. The teaching of the tīrthikas is this: through the cutting off of the grasping of objects, consciousness is cut off; and through the cutting off of the continuum of consciousness, the beginningless continuum, too, is cut off."

"Apart from the cause of which they speak, saying that the continuum arises from a cause, the tīrthikas do not say that eye consciousness arises from the coming together of form and light. The cause they speak of is primordial matter, the person, the Lord, time or atoms.

"Mahāmati, those who possess the application through which great compassion and skill in means come to be spontaneously accomplished, who see all the realms of beings as an illusion, as a reflection, as sameness, as not initiated by conditions, and as isolated from inner and outer activity, and who, through viewing what is external as mind, possess the abode of signlessness, gradually follow the sequence of the grounds that are the domain of meditative absorption; and by meditating thoroughly with strong conviction that the minds of the three realms are an illusion, they attain the illusion-like meditative absorption.

"Through entering into the mere appearance of their own minds, the bodhisattvas who have attained abiding in the perfection of wisdom and are free of the activity of producing attain the meditative absorption that is like a vajra image. Following after the body of the Tathāgata, endowed with its emanations, adorned with the powers, the superknowledges, compassion and means, reaching all buddhafields and all the abodes of the tīrthikas, free of mind, mentation and consciousness, and transformed in their basis step by step, they fully attain the body of the Tathāgata.

Just as various illusions
Appear and yet do not exist,
So too the Tathāgata's various
Teachings are an illusory display.

What is taught to one
Is not fit to be told to another.
Just as a physician
Gives different medicines to different patients,
So the Buddha has taught beings
Even that there is mind alone.

The domain of individual self-knowing
That the protectors have taught
Is not the domain of logicians,
Nor is it that of the śrāvakas either.

"Mahāmati, moreover, a bodhisattva who wishes to understand fully the domain of conceptual thought, of grasped and grasper, which are the appearances of his own mind, should be free of gatherings, busyness, sleep and the obscurations. He should strive in the practice of not sleeping in the first, last and middle watches of the night. He should keep apart from the treatises and the talk of evil tīrthikas and from the characteristics of the śrāvaka and pratyekabuddha vehicles; and the great bodhisattva should realize, so as to understand it, the characteristic of conceptual thought with regard to the appearances of his own mind.

The various appearances do not exist;
Mind arises from appearance.
As body, enjoyments and dwelling place,
The ground consciousness appears to people."

Mahāmati made a further request for the sake of purifying the stream of the appearances of one's own mind: "Blessed One, is the stream of the appearances of one's own mind purified all at once, or by entering into it gradually? How does it become purified?" He said: "Gradually, not all at once," and he gave as examples the ripening of mango fruit, a potter making vessels, plants growing from the earth, and skill in music developing gradually. Then, with the example of a reflection appearing in a mirror all at once and without conceptual thought, he taught that the Tathāgata, too, purifies all at once the stream of the appearances of beings' own minds into a domain free of conceptual thought and free of appearance.

"Mahāmati, what, again, is the śrāvakas' clinging to the nature of imagined things? It is this: having seen the great elements, blue, yellow, warm, wet, moving and solid, arising without a maker, and having seen their own and general characteristics, reasoning, the valid cognition of scripture, and the extensive commentaries, there arises conceptual thought that clings to their nature. Mahāmati, a bodhisattva should understand this and turn away from it."

Then Mahāmati again said to the Blessed One: "Please explain to me the characteristics of superimposition and denial, so that I and other bodhisattvas, with minds that have abandoned the evil views of superimposition and denial, may awaken to unsurpassed awakening, and, having awakened, may abandon the views of permanence and superimposition, of annihilation and denial, and so not disparage the way of you, the Buddha." He said:

"Superimposition and denial
Do not exist in mind alone.
Those who do not know the mind
As body, enjoyments and dwelling place,
Those unskilled ones engage
In superimposition and denial."

"Without intrinsic nature" is said with the intent that nothing is freed by an intrinsic nature of its own; thus all things are called without intrinsic nature.

"Mahāmati, the characteristic of arising in dependence on causes, which is the support of all, is of two kinds: outer and inner. Outer dependent origination is this: a pot arises through such conditions as a lump of clay, a stick, a wheel, string, water and human effort; likewise cloth from weaving, a mat from grass, a sprout from a seed, and butter from curd through a churning stick and human effort. This is outer dependent origination, in which the later arises from the earlier. The inner is this: ignorance, existence, karma; these and other such phenomena receive the name 'dependent origination.' Mahāmati, the phenomena born from these, called aggregates, elements and sense sources, also receive the name 'dependent origination.' From what is without distinction, these are imagined by the childish."

Mahāmati said: "Blessed One, in explaining dependent origination, the Blessed One has simply spoken of causes, but has not taught the way in which things dissolve again according to his own system. Blessed One, the tīrthikas too say that things arise from a cause, namely from the working of primordial matter, the Lord, the person, time and atoms. The Blessed One declares that things arise using different terms, but there is no distinction in the tenet itself."

"Blessed One, the tīrthikas too speak of the nonexistent and of arising from the nonexistent, and the Blessed One too says that things arise from the nonexistent through conditions and, having arisen, perish. And what the Blessed One has taught, 'conditioned by ignorance,' and so on up to old age and death, is taught in the manner of those who speak of no cause; it is not the speech of those who speak of a cause. This saying, 'When this exists, that exists,' fits things that abide simultaneously, but not things that abide in dependence on arising in sequence. The tīrthikas' account itself is the better; yours, Blessed One, is not good. Why is that? The tīrthikas bring forth results through causes that are not dependently originated. In yours, Blessed One, the cause depends on the result and the result depends on the cause, so that cause and condition are mixed together, and likewise there comes to be no end to their mutual dependence. By saying, 'When this exists, that exists,' the world would become causeless."

He said: "Mahāmati, since it is understood that there is no grasped and no grasper, and that everything is the mere appearance of one's own mind, even when one says 'this exists,' it does not become a doctrine of cause and agent, nor does cause and condition become mixed together. This fault arises for those who cling to grasped and grasper and do not understand that, there being no external thing that is an object in its own right, everything is the mere appearance of one's own mind; it does not arise for my teaching of dependent origination.

"Mahāmati, an illusion arises through the power of another person's spell, not from the power of the habitual tendencies of the dysfunction of one's own conceptual thought. It was for the benefit of childish ordinary beings, whose minds have fallen into the marks of evil views, who do not realize that everything is the mere appearance of their own minds, and who cling to the characteristic of arising from the activity of causes and conditions, that all phenomena were taught as having the character of illusions and dreams.

"Mahāmati, all phenomena are without essential nature. Why is that? Mahāmati, when one examines them with one's own intelligence, things with particular and general characteristics are not tenable; therefore all phenomena are said to be without essential nature."

When asked whether the life principle and the body are different or not different, and so on, it is the tīrthikas, who do not know that there is mind alone, who set the question aside; it is not so with the Buddha. The Buddha's answer by setting aside is for those without wisdom: in consideration of the vessel, the question is for the time being left unanswered.

"Through clinging to the false as true, the assemblage of the five aggregates and the derivatives of the elements arise. Mahāmati, consciousness, too, through clinging to and desiring the various objects of its abode, comes to link into rebirth in other destinies. The derivatives of the earth element and the rest have the great elements as their cause, but the great elements themselves have none. Why is that? Mahāmati, since there is no substance, mark, characteristic, shape, nature of activity, or application, the elements do not arise. Things arise from the coming together of the activities of substance and so on, not from what lacks marks. Therefore, Mahāmati, the characteristics of the elements and of the derivatives of the elements are imagined by the tīrthikas, not by me.

The various appearances of the imagined
Are called the dependent;
If they are imagined in some other way,
One comes to abide in the tīrthikas' doctrine.
Conception is called the conceived;
Because it is seen, it arises from causes;
What is free of the two kinds of conceptual thought
Is itself the perfected.

"Mahāmati, because all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas have not abandoned the obscuration of knowables and the habitual tendencies of karma, they do not realize the selflessness of phenomena, and they have not attained the inconceivable death and transference. For that reason three vehicles were taught to the śrāvakas, and not one. Mahāmati, when they have abandoned all the habitual tendencies of faults and realized the selflessness of phenomena, then those who had become intoxicated by the faults of habitual tendencies and by meditative absorption awaken again from the undefiled sphere, fully complete the accumulations belonging to the worldly and the transworldly undefiled sphere, and fully attain mastery over the inconceivable Dharma body.

Though there is no establishment of vehicles,
In order to guide beings
I have taught different vehicles;
I have taught a single vehicle.

"When the basis of the mind has been thoroughly transformed, and the other gateways of the illusion-like meditative absorption have been fully attained, there is a body that blossoms with the flowers of many marks, masteries and superknowledges, swift as the mind, like an illusion, a dream or a reflection, not made of the elements yet resembling the elements and their derivatives, gathering together the various limbs of every form, and going into the circle of the assembly in all the buddhafields. Because it realizes the essential nature of phenomena, it is called a body.

Turning back all views,
Abandoning conception and the conceived,
When there is no object and no arising,
I have taught that too as mind alone.

Not a thing and not a non-thing,
Abandoning both thing and non-thing;
Likewise liberation from the mind
I have taught too as mind alone.

Suchness and the limit of emptiness,
Nirvāṇa and the sphere of phenomena,
The various mind-made bodies:
I have taught these too as mind alone.

Bound up with the habitual tendencies of conceptual thought,
The various arise from the mind;
To people it appears as external;
The world is mind alone.

What appears as external does not exist at all;
The mind appears as the various.
Like body, enjoyments and dwelling place,
I have taught it to be mind alone."

In the presence of the Buddha, a brahmin who was a Lokāyata materialist asked many questions, such as, "Is everything made or not made? Is it permanent or impermanent? Is it one or different?" The Buddha said in reply: "All that is said in such terms is Lokāyata. O brahmin, this Lokāyata is yours; it is not his."

"Brahmin, I teach that the three existences have arisen from the cause that is the dysfunctional habitual tendencies of elaborating conceptual thought from beginningless time. Brahmin, conceptual thought arises from not understanding that there is only the appearance of one's own mind, not from apprehending external things. Thus a meeting of three, self, faculty and object, such as the tīrthikas teach, is not mine. O brahmin, I am neither one who speaks of causes nor one who speaks of no cause. Rather, having thoroughly demolished the things that are grasped and grasper, which are conceptual thought itself, I teach dependent origination. Those like you, and others whose mental streams have fallen into grasping at a self, do not understand it."

"Moreover, Mahāmati, a Lokāyata brahmin said this to me: 'O Gautama, have these three existences arisen from the causes of ignorance, craving and karma, or have they not arisen from causes?' Brahmin, both of these are Lokāyata. 'O Gautama, do all things fall under particular and general characteristics?' O brahmin, that too is Lokāyata. Brahmin, insofar as there is stirring from the mind and conceptual thought clinging to external objects, to that extent there is Lokāyata.

Just as forms in a mirror
Appear and yet do not exist,
So in the mirror of habitual tendencies
The childish see the mind as twofold.
Through not knowing the appearances of the mind,
Twofold conceptual thought arises;
When the appearances of the mind are fully known,
Conceptual thought does not arise.

The mind takes on various forms,
Yet it is free of mark and basis of mark;
Though the childish imagine it so,
The aspect of appearance does not appear.
Since the three existences are mere conceptual thought,
There are no external objects;
That the various appearances are conceptual thought
The childish do not understand.

As long as there are assertions,
So long is everything confused.
Look upon it as one's own mind alone,
And do not go seeking disputes.

"Mahāmati, if the tathāgatagarbha, which is proclaimed as the ground consciousness, did not exist, then, since there would be no tathāgatagarbha proclaimed as the ground consciousness, there would be neither engagement nor turning back. But Mahāmati, for the childish and for the noble ones there is both engagement and turning back; and yogins who have no wish for striving abide in the happiness of this life, the individual self-knowing of their own.

As warm, wet, solid and moving
The childish imagine phenomena;
It is the superimposition of the unreal;
There is no mark and no basis of mark.

In this body of continua
There is form made of eight substances;
In the shape of the body and in the faculties
The childish go astray in conceptual thought.

Body, enjoyments and dwelling place
Are the three representations of the grasped;
Mentation, mental consciousness
And conceptual thought are the three graspers.

Through clinging to one's own mind,
Mind arises in abundance.
What appears as external does not exist at all;
For that reason there is mind alone.

The faculties are like an illusion,
And the objects resemble a dream;
Agent, action and activity
Do not stir in any way at all.
Conventionally, everything exists;
Ultimately, it does not exist.

This that appears as the various
Is the mind imprinted by dysfunction,
Grasping at what appears as form;
The mind is deluded about externals.

Appearance is like a rabbit's horn,
Yet people have conceptual thought;
Like the deer before a mirage,
Those who conceive of it are mistaken.
Through clinging to conceptual thought,
Conceptual thought arises intensely.

The external is the imagined nature;
The dependent arises from its own basis.
Whoever does not see the delusion arisen in himself,
His minds are utterly destroyed.

Having that as its cause, it takes that as its object;
It is the abode in which mentation moves.
It also brings forth the cause of the mind,
And consciousness, too, abides in it.

As long as one conceives of external things
As possessing characteristics,
And does not see one's own mistake,
So long does one make the mind expand.

The mind is not divided by habitual tendencies,
Yet mind and habitual tendencies are not one;
The mind, whose mark is undivided,
Is wrapped all around by habitual tendencies.
Regard the habitual tendencies arising from mental consciousness
As like a stain;
The mind is like a white cloth,
Made unlovely by the habitual tendencies."

From the section on nirvāṇa: He asked: "Does the Blessed One establish eight consciousnesses?" "I do establish them." "If you establish them, how is it that mental consciousness is turned back, while the others are not so set forth?" He said: "Because of its cause and its object, the assembled consciousnesses arise. Mahāmati, mental consciousness, arising with clinging to the discrimination of objects, greatly nourishes the ground consciousness of habitual tendencies. Together with mentation, through clinging to grasping at I and mine, it follows along as if thinking of itself; with the ground consciousness as its cause and with that as its object, its characteristic is that of a body that is not different. Through clinging to objects that are appearances of one's own mind, the assembly of minds arises, each one becoming the cause of the next. Mahāmati, like the great ocean, stirred by the wind of objects that are appearances of one's own mind, they arise and subside. Therefore, through turning back mental consciousness, the seven also turn back.

Relying on the ground consciousness,
Mentation comes into play;
Relying on mind and mentation,
Consciousness comes into play.

On white, a stain appears;
On a stain, the mind does not appear.
Just as with clouds in the sky,
So too the mind does not appear.

Not all form is made of the elements;
There is form not made of the elements:
The city of the gandharvas, a dream,
An illusion and a mirage are not made of the elements.

Just as gold is refined,
And water that was turbid becomes clear,
And as the sky is without clouds,
So is conceptual thought purified.

The middle, undestroyed, and mind alone;
What is not the middle arises as a continuum.

In the combination of qualities, atoms and substances
The childish conceive of form;
It does not exist in any single atom,
And therefore, too, there is no object.

The shape that is the appearance of one's own mind
Appears to people as external;
What appears as external does not exist,
And therefore, too, there is no object."

Tangled hairs and an illusion,
A dream and a city of gandharvas,
A firebrand's wheel, a mirage, and all such things,
Though they do not exist, appear vividly to people.

Mind, conceptual thought and cognizance,
Mentation and consciousness itself,
The all-ground, and the stirrings of the three realms of existence:
These are synonyms of mind.

What the petty śrāvakas
And the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) have composed,
Because it is expounded by mere reasoning,
They are mistaken about its meaning.

From the Dharmasaṃgīti Sūtra: "Through ten ways of entering the Dharma, bodhisattvas should understand non-association. What are the ten? They are as follows: not associating with inferior beings who think of the inferior and aspire to the inferior; not associating with beings who make no effort, who are unenterprising, lazy and without diligence; not associating with beings who are puffed up with the seven kinds of pride and with arrogance; not associating with beings whose nature is stinginess, corrupt moral discipline, malice, laziness, distraction and corrupt wisdom; not associating with thoughts of sensual desire, malice and harmfulness; not associating with any thinking, conceptualizing or imagining; not associating with any obscurations, hindrances or entanglements; not associating with any of the mental attention of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas; not associating with any mental attention that delights in gain, honor and fame; and not associating with clinging to 'I' and clinging to 'mine.'"

It is said that in a rainbow the colors gray, blue and red are, in order, the dominance of earth, water and fire, and the bow shape is the dominance of wind.

When a monk who practices yoga, whose accumulation of what is not virtuous has been purified, has fully attained mastery, then by merely thinking of the totality as space it becomes so: it becomes free of the obstruction of solidity, moisture, heat and motion, and of blue, yellow, red and white. Yet the unobstructedness of mind does not pass over into the unobstructedness of space, nor does a thing, by mere thought, become free of solidity and the rest without being possessed of unobstructedness. In the same way, one should understand the very power of the karmic maturation of all virtuous and nonvirtuous actions arisen from conditions.

Concerning the eight liberations of a bodhisattva: "One with form sees forms" means that one sees them as dependent origination, as empty and as unmoving; because one is freed by this, it is called liberation. "Perceiving no form internally, one sees external forms" means seeing them as emptiness, as dependent origination and as unmoving. "Being intent on the beautiful" is emptiness; the unbeautiful is what has turned into a view; so it is by being intent on the beautiful. "Having passed beyond perceptions of form, thinking 'Space is infinite'" is emptiness. In "thinking 'Consciousness is infinite,'" consciousness is explained as being beginningless, and whatever is beginningless is empty; so it is by seeing it thus. In "thinking 'There is nothing at all,'" "anything" means the three times, and one sees them as nonexistent. "Neither perception nor non-perception" is to be perfected and abided in: "without perception" means emptiness, and "the sphere of not without perception" means dependent origination; so it is by seeing it thus. As for the cessation of perception and feeling, one thinks: "Perception is like a mirage, and feeling is like a bubble on water. Perception itself is feeling and feeling itself is perception, so here there is no knower and no feeler," and so on; so it is by seeing it thus. Such is the cessation of perception and feeling.

Concerning the ten totality spheres of a bodhisattva: if one is intent on any totality, from the earth totality up to the consciousness totality, it becomes just so; therefore they are called totality spheres. And because each one becomes all of the spheres, they are therefore called totality spheres.

Concerning the ten masteries of a bodhisattva: by attaining the deathless, there is mastery over life. Because one fully knows everything as mind only, there is mastery over mind. Because one has gained the jewel in the hand and the sky treasury, there is mastery over requisites. By cutting off the paths of karma together with their habitual tendencies, there is mastery over karma. By finding the meditative concentration that turns back from meditative absorption, there is mastery over birth. By attaining entry into what is to be spontaneously accomplished, there is mastery over miraculous power. By attaining the totality spheres, there is mastery over resolve. By achieving one's aims through mere thought, there is mastery over aspiration. By attaining actions of body, speech and mind that are preceded by wisdom, there is mastery over wisdom. Because of the stainless wisdom that is the equality of suchness, the realm of phenomena and the limit of reality, there is mastery over the Dharma. Which discordant factors the ten masteries are the antidotes to, and their causes as well, are explained in this Dharma text.

"A bodhisattva is one who is restrained by the vow of individual liberation, and yet does not think that one is made pure by the prātimokṣa alone, but relies on the bodhisattva vow." In this Dharma text the ten buddhas are found.

From the Sūtra Teaching the Great Miracle: "Mañjuśrī, how is a bodhisattva pure in body?" He replied: "Son of the gods, it is when, with a mind-made body, one displays oneself in conformity with the bodies of all sentient beings. How is one pure in speech? It is when, by fully perfecting the sounds of all sentient beings, one teaches the Dharma fruitfully. How is one pure in mind? It is when, with a single mind, one fully knows the minds of all sentient beings."

In the presence of the Buddha, the noble Mañjuśrī and the son of the gods the Caravan Leader were discussing the Dharma, and the Caravan Leader spoke much profound Dharma. Śāriputra said to the Buddha, "This Caravan Leader possesses roots of virtue." From the account that he had increased his virtue in the past as well: countless eons ago, in the world system called Happiness, when the Buddha Sumerukalpa appeared, there was a universal monarch called Array of Virtue. The Buddha taught him the Dharma, and he, together with his sons, attained the acceptance that conforms to the Dharma. Then in that assembly there was a monk, a preacher of the Dharma, called Dharmadhvaja, who explained the profound Dharma to the king; and the king, delighted, gave him as an offering whatever clothes and ornaments he had.

Then that monk said that clothes and the like are not a great offering, but that going forth and practicing the Dharma is the offering, and so on, up to:

Were all the beings in a great trichiliocosm
To set out for the sake of awakening,
And with undaunted minds, for a full eon,
To give away their children and their wives,
Then one who, having roused the mind for the sake of awakening,
In order to train in the footsteps of the Tathāgata,
Takes, Subhūti, but a single step with that intent,
Has merit far surpassing theirs.

The buddhas who arose in the past,
And those not yet come, and those who now abide:
None of them, without forsaking the household of many faults,
Attained unsurpassed and peaceful awakening.

Going forth is taught, praised and honored
By all the tathāgatas;
Whoever wishes to make offering to all the buddhas,
Take up going forth in the Victor's teaching.

If one who dwells in a household gives buddha-fields
As many as the Ganges' sands, adorned with jeweled houses,
And one who has gone forth, for just a single night,
Contemplates impermanence, suffering and no-self,

The bodhisattva who has gone forth and become conscientious
Is near to awakening,
Has utterly destroyed the circle of Māra,
And the side of virtue does not wane but grows.

That going forth has forsaken the evil paths,
Accords with the path, and is praised by the noble ones;
It is no small virtue,
And it is the forsaking of possessiveness.

Freed from the household, afflictions do not grow;
Freed from the afflictions, there is no bondage of Māra;
The liberated mind practices the conduct of awakening;
Through pure conduct, awakening is not hard to find.

Then the king, wishing to go forth, made his request to the Buddha, and when it was granted he said to his thousand sons, "Which of you is willing to hold the kingdom and to protect beings in accordance with the Dharma?" All of them said, "I too shall go forth; please do not leave me behind." The king said, "If you all go forth in this way, the beings of the four continents will come to ruin. Whichever of you is steadfast in compassion, take up the kingdom and bring beings into the Dharma." Then one prince, with a compassionate mind, consented, and all the others went forth. At that time the king was the Caravan Leader, and the Dharma-preaching monk was Mañjuśrī. The prince who held the kingdom was the Blessed One Śākyamuni, and the other princes are the other buddhas of the Fortunate Eon.

From the Jñānālokālaṃkāra Sūtra: "Suppose, for example, that this great earth turned into the nature of beryl, and the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, with Śakra and his enjoyments, appeared in it. The gods would say to the people of Jambudvīpa, 'O, look at the lord of the gods! You too, through merit, should act so as to become like this!' And they would perform merit and also make aspirations. Although Śakra and the rest are not there, it appears so because the ground is pure. In the same way, because they are thoroughly familiarized with meditation and their minds are pure, sentient beings come to see the body of the Tathāgata, and they see that body through the blessing of the Tathāgata. And although it is unborn and unceasing, neither appearing nor not appearing, still, focusing on the reflection of the Tathāgata's body, they make merit and dedicate it. The reflection of Śakra does not move, does not think and does not conceptualize; likewise the Tathāgata too is not born and does not conceptualize, yet he appears to the world like a reflection. Because the ranks of sentient beings' vision differ, he shows differing degrees of greatness. Through ripening and through generating the power of faith, they come to hear the Dharma, and, according to their dispositions, they come to be liberated through wisdom in the three vehicles. Because of the former ripening of beginner bodhisattvas, and also of childish ordinary beings who are to be trained by his passing into nirvāṇa, he ceases to appear like a reflection, and they think, 'The Tathāgata has passed into nirvāṇa.' Just as the disk of the sun rises in sequence over high and low ground, so the sun disk of the Tathāgata's wisdom also rises in sequence, from bodhisattvas down to even those who have entered wrong ways. In brief, all unwholesome mental attention is the cause of thorough affliction, and all wholesome mental attention is the cause of purification."

He declared, "The Tathāgata has attained an awakening that is without root and without basis." Mañjuśrī asked, "What are the root and the basis?" "The root is the view of the perishable collection; the basis is the imputation of what is unreal. The meaning that is inexpressible is the meaning of dependent origination. The meaning of dependent origination is the meaning of the Dharma. The meaning of the Dharma is the meaning of the Buddha. Therefore whoever sees dependent origination sees the Dharma, and whoever sees the Dharma sees the Buddha; and one sees in such a way that, when one searches, one sees nothing at all. Mañjuśrī, what is meant by 'anything'? It is mind and its object. So when one does not see mind and object, one sees what is real. In this way the Tathāgata has fully and perfectly awakened to those phenomena as equal through their equality."

Through undefiled virtuous qualities the Tathāgata
Is like a reflection; in this
There is no Tathāgata, nor is there suchness;
It is only a reflection appearing in the worlds.

From the Dharmasaṃgīti Sūtra: "Concerning the nine successive meditative attainments of a bodhisattva: secluded from sense desires, secluded from evil and unwholesome phenomena, with thought and with analysis, possessed of the joy and bliss born of seclusion, one perfects and abides in the first meditative concentration. Here sense desires are the side discordant with the first meditative concentration, and one is secluded from those that one craves. The evil and unwholesome phenomena are attachment, aversion and delusion; from that basis the ten unwholesome actions arise. These are called evil and unwholesome phenomena, and seclusion from them is the first meditative concentration. Being accompanied by thought is being 'with thought.' What are those thoughts? They are the thoughts that accord with the first meditative concentration. Because one thinks in accordance with the proper procedure, and because they too are tamed, they are called thoughts: knowing, comprehending, analyzing, examining closely and bringing about absorption, these are thought."

What is analysis? Analyzing, investigating, closely analyzing, fully savoring, bringing about absorption, and the mind's following after those very thoughts that accord with the first meditative concentration: these are called analysis. Being accompanied by them is being "with thought and with analysis"; this is the first meditative concentration. Because the joy and bliss are born of seclusion, they are therefore called "the joy and bliss born of seclusion." In "one perfects and abides," "perfects" means restraining and causing restraint, guarding and causing it to be guarded, savoring and delighting in it. The bodhisattva abiding in that meditative concentration gains the resolve and the wish to realize the acceptance that phenomena are unborn, and through that resolve and wish does not regard the first meditative concentration as the essence.

The bodhisattva then undertakes the second meditative concentration, pacifying those thoughts and analyses of the first meditative concentration and calming the mind completely from them, and seeks the cause for accomplishing the second meditative concentration. Free of thought and analysis, inwardly fully clear, the mind having become a single continuum, without thought and without analysis, endowed with joy, one perfects and abides in the second meditative concentration. Here "inwardly fully clear" means being endowed with the pacification of whatever phenomena are the side discordant with that second meditative concentration; therefore it is called "inwardly fully clear." "Having become a single continuum" means that, all those thoughts and analyses of the first meditative concentration having been fully pacified, one attains the state of becoming of a single taste, like the water of the ocean. Just as, for example, the waters that flow into the great ocean become of a single taste in its salt, so for the bodhisattva absorbed in the second meditative concentration all those thoughts and analyses of the first meditative concentration are pacified and become of a single taste; that is called meditative absorption. From that meditative absorption joy arises in the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha, and through that joy in the Jewels, wholesome qualities come to completion. From that one attains a resolve to attain the acceptance that phenomena are unborn, which is exceedingly blazing, exceedingly luminous and exceedingly radiant.

With that resolve gathered, the bodhisattva is not attached to the second meditative concentration and seeks out the third meditative concentration, thinking, "Joy is an obscuration to the third meditative concentration." Then, through detachment from that joy, with equanimity and mindfulness fully purified, one perfects and abides in the third meditative concentration, which the noble ones describe as "abiding in bliss without joy." Having attained freedom from attachment to the joy born of meditative absorption, one attains a mind such as this: "This bliss without joy is not lasting bliss; it is subject to exhaustion and destruction. It is impermanent, unstable and not everlasting, and it cannot be relied upon." Having understood this, one attains a great and intense resolve to fully attain the acceptance that phenomena are unborn. Through that intense resolve, clinging to bliss and suffering does not arise. Abandoning bliss as well, having already abandoned suffering, and with mental pleasure and mental displeasure also having subsided, neither blissful nor suffering, with equanimity and mindfulness fully purified, one perfects and abides in the fourth meditative concentration.

When the bodhisattva is absorbed in that fourth meditative concentration, the mind is a mind of the antidote, a pleasing mind, a workable mind, a luminous mind, a mindful mind. All sentient beings long for bliss; and being turned toward the benefit and bliss of all sentient beings, the bodhisattva gives rise to the light of the acceptance that phenomena are unborn. Having given rise to that light, the bodhisattva takes no delight in that fourth meditative concentration, so full of what is neither suffering nor bliss, and does not become attached even to the bliss of purified equanimity and mindfulness. Being unattached to it, it comes to appear as space itself. Passing completely beyond all perceptions of form, with perceptions of resistance having subsided, and not attending to perceptions of diversity, thinking "Space is infinite," one perfects and abides in the sphere of infinite space.

The bodhisattva thinks thus: "In brief, form here is of two kinds: the elements and what derives from the elements. The form of the elements is the four great elements; what derives from the elements is color, smell, taste and touch. Thus form is eightfold." Having passed completely beyond the perceptions of those forms, perceptions of resistance do not arise; they subside. Wherever there is perception of form, there is perception of resistance; therefore, having passed completely beyond perceptions of form, perceptions of resistance subside. When perceptions of resistance have subsided, by not attending to perceptions of diversity one enters into the thought "Space is infinite." Then, thinking "Space is infinite," one perfects and abides in the sphere of infinite space.

Here the bodhisattva absorbed in the sphere of infinite space thinks thus: "Space has no end. How could what has no end have a beginning? How could it have a middle? In the same way, all phenomena are without beginning, end or middle."
