From Maitreya's Great Lion's Roar: There are four thieves in the guise of ascetics: the monk who keeps the color, the sign and the form of an ascetic yet has broken discipline and is possessed of evil qualities; the one who has gone to a solitary place yet dwells in unwholesome conceptual thoughts; the one who, although he has not attained the fruit, claims to have attained the fruit out of attachment to gain and the like; and the one who praises himself and disparages others.

He declared: "Kāśyapa, before long the Tathāgata will pass into parinirvāṇa." Kāśyapa requested: "In order to protect the true Dharma, please remain for more than an eon." He declared: "Even if as many as a thousand buddhas were to teach the Dharma accompanied by miracles, those ignoble people who hold to evil could not be held back from it or turned away from it."

He declared: "Maitreya, in the last five hundred years of the later time there will appear those who claim to be bodhisattvas yet behave entirely in the manner of dogs. When they see another monk coming to a household that they themselves used to visit, they look at him with anger and say such things as, 'He has this fault.' For the sake of a few morsels of food, which become a cause of vomit, which do not last long and which are to be despised, they produce a great deal of demerit. They behave entirely in the manner of hungry ghosts; they are vexatious and possessed of a mean mind. In name they claim to be bodhisattvas, and while they themselves harbor evil intentions, they speak the praises of the buddhas and bodhisattvas and so procure food and drink.

"Maitreya, just as a jackal might roar a lion's roar yet do a jackal's work, so they speak the praises of the four means of gathering disciples, yet because they practice giving and the rest toward no sentient being at all, it remains mere words; they do not strive in conduct bound up with truly taking up good qualities. They do not rejoice in their own gain as much as they rejoice when others fail to gain. Seeing how unhappy they become at the gain of others, consider how perverse they would become if they had to give away to others all their own means of comfort without regard for them!

"A monk who goes among households should not, apart from the gift of the Dharma, talk of material goods or of worldly bustle, for these are of no benefit." With this in mind it was taught:
Whoever fills world-systems as many as the sands of the Ganges
with supreme jewels and offers them to the Victor with a mind of supreme joy,
and whoever gives a single verse to one sentient being:
that exceedingly vast gift of jewels
cannot match even a fraction of the gift of a verse brought about by compassion,
nor does it even enter into the reckoning;
how then could one think of comparing it with two or three?

Those of the bodhisattva vehicle, having purified their karma, will not be born in the lower realms through minor nonvirtuous actions. Even so, since they wish to free others by being free themselves, they should be scrupulous and cautious, should purify their faults quickly, and should regard the world beyond with fear.

Here, where there is no self, no sentient being,
no life principle and no person,
whatever is the unsurpassed acceptance of this,
that giving is the great giving.
One should purify a moral discipline
that does not stray from the way of peace.
Whoever realizes the beauty of the way of peace,
that, it is said, is the supreme discipline.
Strive also with steadfast diligence,
and abandon everything conditioned.
Whatever sees solitude,
that the Buddha called diligence.
Since entities have no entityhood,
one should burn up all entities.
Whatever is free of affliction,
that is concentration without conceptual thought.
Not on this shore, not on the far shore,
nor should one remain in the middle;
whatever sees the three times as equal,
that is the sublime wisdom.

By the great elder Kāśyapa:
Not on this shore, not on the far shore,
nor should one remain in the middle;
whatever sees the three times as equal,
that is the sublime wisdom.

The great elder Kāśyapa requested: "For the sake of benefiting many people, please explain the signs by which bodhisattvas of weak superior intention will be known to be hypocrites." He declared: "In the last five hundred years of the later time, those bodhisattvas who are in the grip of evil friends and whose superior intention is weak will busy themselves with acts of worship to the relics of the Tathāgata, from flowers up to lamps."

"Kāśyapa, when I teach the worship by which those without understanding properly establish roots of virtue, those foolish people become zealous in that alone. When I have declared before the world with its gods, 'You should dwell striving after taming yourselves and after application to peace; if there are faithful brahmins and householders, they will make offerings to my relics,' look at how those foolish people abandon yoga, renunciation, receiving the scriptures and recitation, and make their living by depending on the worship of relics!

"Suppose someone were to fill everything from the mass of waters up to the world of Brahmā with sesame oil, set up a lamp with a wick the size of Sumeru, and so make offerings to the Tathāgata. Far more merit than that is produced by a bodhisattva of superior intention who abides in moral discipline, receives from a preceptor or a master a single verse of four lines, recites it, and takes seven steps.

"For one who perceives in terms of 'buddha', there is not even a seeing of the Buddha, so what need is there to speak of the act of worship? That is impossible. Whoever does not deal in perceptions and signs, that one worships the Tathāgata. Friends, since the body of the Tathāgata is unborn, it is not easy to worship.

"Kāśyapa, in the last five hundred years of the later time there will appear followers of the bodhisattva vehicle who are unskilled in means and overpowered by greed and desire. They think, and will come to think, that by making offerings to painted cloths, to paintings on walls or to other images of the Tathāgata they will attain accomplishments and miraculous powers. With that mere trifling root of virtue they praise themselves and disparage others, and they think to make their living on that basis. At that time, apart from making images of the Tathāgata and worshiping them, they will not cherish receiving the scriptures, recitation or meditative concentration. At that time, known by householders and renunciants for their offerings and their paintings, they will obtain alms, medicine and every requisite besides.

"When I teach that the work of followers of the bodhisattva vehicle is this, namely, 'Abiding in moral discipline, one should seek much learning,' look at how at that time they will dwell in broken discipline, abandon the search for the Dharma, busy themselves with acts of worship at stūpas, and think to make their living on that basis!"

From the Great Parinirvāṇa: Furthermore, apart from King Ajātaśatru, there arrived the king of Vaiśālī, Stainless Moon Essence, together with his servants and retinue dwelling within the city and without, and with kings of all the cities, mountain villages, great provinces and valleys of Jambudvīpa, as many as the sands of six Ganges rivers. Furthermore, at that time, apart from the queen of King Ajātaśatru, there arrived the queen of the king of Vaiśālī, Beauty of the Three Worlds, together with the wives of the kings, merchants and householders of Jambudvīpa, as many as the sands of the Ganges river.

Here, "self" means "the Buddha". "Permanent" means "the Dharma body". "Bliss" means nirvāṇa. "Pure" is a synonym for the Dharma. Thus, among all permanent things, space is supremely permanent, and the Tathāgata too is permanent like space.

Although I had already reached the culmination of arhatship countless eons ago, when I showed myself seated on grass spread at the foot of the tree of awakening and taming Māra, sentient beings understood it thus: "In order to deliver the world, he has defeated Māra and become a buddha." Yet I defeated Māra countless eons ago and realized the deathless.

"Highest, highest": just as among all the directions the north is supreme, so too is liberation; and whatever is liberation, that is the Tathāgata. "Unsurpassed, unsurpassed": just as among all lands the northern one is called the unsurpassed land, so too is liberation; and that is the Tathāgata. "River" refers to the ocean: among all rivers, the ocean alone is the river, and so the ocean is counted in the category "river".

Those sentient beings who possess immeasurable good qualities will have faith on hearing the Great Vehicle. For those sons of good family who long for the true Dharma, the countless evil deeds they have committed will be cleansed by no more than a headache, being tormented by fever, a stabbing pain, or slander.

"Blessed One, how is one to understand the one possessed of moral discipline and the one of broken discipline spoken of in the sūtra on the likeness of the four kinds of fruit?"

"Dharma" means the nature of reality: the Tathāgata is called permanent, stable and everlasting. If persons of broken discipline who are greedy for material things say, "The Tathāgata is impermanent," the point is that those persons are not authoritative. The sūtras of definitive meaning are those of the Great Vehicle; those of the śrāvaka vehicle belong to the category of provisional meaning. "Wisdom" is a synonym for the Tathāgata: the Dharma body is authoritative. The statement "the Tathāgata is a body of flesh" rests on what consciousness imagines, and consciousness is not authoritative. "Meaning": "the Tathāgata is permanent" means "the Dharma is permanent", and "the Dharma is permanent" means "the Saṅgha is permanent". "Letter" means self-made letters and the various words not spoken by the Buddha. "Self" means the tathāgatagarbha.

The teaching of the Buddha is supremely vast and extremely subtle: it is the supreme nectar, and it is also the supreme poison. The Buddha himself is also the Dharma and also the Saṅgha: the Tathāgata himself is the threefold basis. In the śrāvaka vehicle the three bases are set out in order to dispel the worldly views of childish beings.

Heedfulness is the abode of the deathless; heedlessness is the abode of death. Worldly people are possessed of heedlessness and are like the dead.

What has passed beyond the world has no aging and no death. The heedful attain nirvāṇa, the supreme bliss.

The body of the Tathāgata and his words of hidden intent are hard to understand. For example, when a king says to his minister, "Bring saindhava!", at mealtime the minister brings salt; when the meal is over he offers water to drink; when the king is going to the pleasure grove, a horse; and when he is going to battle, he brings a sword. In this way he understands the king's words of intent. Just so in this Great Vehicle, the ministers of the Great Vehicle should understand that there are four kinds of impermanence. When the Tathāgata, having appeared in the world, displays parinirvāṇa, at that time one cultivates the perception of impermanence. When he teaches, "The Dharma will decline and come to an end," at that time one should cultivate suffering. When he teaches, "My body is afflicted by endless illness; the Saṅgha will be destroyed," at that time one cultivates selflessness. When he teaches, "The liberation of the Tathāgata is signless and is nothing at all," at that time one should cultivate emptiness. Because in it there is no suffering whatsoever, and nothing impermanent, unstable, not everlasting or not at peace, the Tathāgata is bliss, permanent, stable, everlasting and at peace. When the Tathāgata explains that all sentient beings possess the tathāgatagarbha, at that time one should cultivate true permanence. Those who cultivate in this way are my śrāvakas; they are skilled in words of hidden intent.

This sūtra teaches the tathāgatagarbha, the nature of a true man. The nature of a true man is what is called "human"; the nature of the Tathāgata is to be a true man.

Son of good family, listen further to what one who has committed a defeat should do. If one who has committed a defeat, having come to understand the suchness of the Dharma, the tathāgatagarbha, thinks, "I shall bring forth the true Dharma," then what is given to him also becomes most excellent. So great is the fruit of bringing forth the true Dharma.

From the Questions of Ugra: "Household": it is called a household because it destroys the roots of virtue and fells the trunk of virtue. "Living in a household": it is called living in a household because conceptual thought about the roots of affliction makes the roots of virtue cultivated in the past decline. Living in a household is as hard to fill as the ocean with streams. Like fire with firewood, it knows no contentment. Like a gale, it is thinking without end. It is a mixture, like good food mixed with poison. It is quarreling over each other's needs. It is work without end. It leads to going to the lower realms. It is suffering, through seeking and guarding possessions; and because these are quickly scattered from one another, in the end it falls into ruin and changes.

When he sees a beggar, he gives rise to the perception that this is a spiritual friend, a cause of wealth, and one who shows the way to awakening. The householder bodhisattva perceives his wife as a net, a trap and a snare; and again in three ways, as a jailer, as bondage and as slaughter. One person should not take the measure of another person; if one does, one will come to ruin.

Dwelling in the wilderness is the holding of all dharmas; it is honored by the noble ones; it is not forgetting the mind of awakening. One who dwells in the wilderness completes the six perfections with little hardship. How so? Generosity, through having no regard for body and life; moral discipline, through the qualities of austere training; patience, through being loving without malice and through patient acceptance of omniscience; diligence, through being irreversible by having attained acceptance of unborn dharmas; meditative concentration, through not abandoning sentient beings and through gathering roots of virtue; and wisdom, through not conceptualizing by means of suchness.

A renunciant bodhisattva who possesses four qualities is permitted to dwell in the wilderness. What are they? He is learned, retaining what he has heard and skilled in establishing the Dharma; he strives at proper attention and practices the Dharma; he is to pacify his strong afflictions; and he has attained the five superknowledges, for the sake of ripening gods, nāgas and others, thinking, "When I have made my roots of virtue firm, I will later teach the Dharma in the villages and elsewhere." He should possess these four.

From the Sūtra on Impermanence:
Health is impermanent, youth does not last,
wealth is impermanent, and life too does not last.
For people menaced by impermanence itself,
how can joy in sense pleasures arise?

From the Kāraṇḍavyūha: In the great city of the land of Vārāṇasī, in a place of excrement and urine, there lived many hundreds of thousands of families of worms. Avalokiteśvara, the Lord, emanated in the form of a bee and said, "Homage to the Buddha." Recollecting the words "Homage to the Buddha," they destroyed with the vajra of wisdom the twenty peaks that had arisen on the mountain of the view of the perishing collection. All of them became bodhisattvas called Sugandhamukha and were born in the world-system Sukhāvatī.

oṃ maṇi padme hūṃ

namaḥ saptānāṃ samyaksaṃbuddhakoṭīnāṃ tadyathā cale cule cunde svāhā

Those who hold the perception of a household toward a monastery will be surrounded by sons and daughters. They make improper use of the Saṅgha's beds, stools, ladles, pillows and mattresses. They defecate and urinate on what belongs to the Saṅgha's use. They also spit, not knowing the maturation of karma. Those who spit on what belongs to the Saṅgha's use are born for twelve years in a sal grove as creatures with mouths the size of a needle's eye. Those who make improper use of the Saṅgha's tooth-sticks are born among turtles, sea monsters and fish. If one makes improper use of the Saṅgha's sesame, rice and the like, one is born as a hungry ghost. Those who feed dogs with the Saṅgha's food are born into low families, with impaired faculties, hunchbacked and destitute. If one makes improper use of what belongs to the Saṅgha, one is born for twelve eons in the Howling hell (Raurava). What belongs to the Saṅgha is like a pit of fire, like poison, like a vajra, like a heavy burden. Poison can be counteracted, but what belongs to the Saṅgha cannot be counteracted.

From the Teaching on Dreams: He declared: "I shall explain to you the aspects, signs and marks by which one is a follower of the bodhisattva vehicle. One should know by one hundred and eight signs that one is a follower of the bodhisattva vehicle: seeing the Tathāgata in a dream, finding relics, making offerings to a stūpa of the Tathāgata, hearing the Dharma, a monk speaking the Dharma, seeing the parasol of the Tathāgata, finding oneself sitting naked, going to a land not seen before, knowing oneself to be in water, knowing that rain is falling from the clouds, the earth shaking, escaping from being killed, going through the sky oneself, finding a book, hearing the name of a Dharma-preaching monk called so-and-so, gathering jewels, the sun and moon seized by Rāhu, knowing oneself to be in a monastery, thunder roaring from the clouds, lightning flashing, seeing that one has found armor and weapons, and so on.

"If a bodhisattva sees in a dream that the Tathāgata says nothing at all, he has accumulated karma that will become an obscuration with regard to the Dharma. He should therefore have others take up listening to the Dharma properly, and should also render service to persons who strive at recitation. By this that obscuration will be purified. That person should be regarded as having come from among the first to the seventh grounds. If at that time he is one who abides on the first ground, then in order to purify it he should recite the Dharma discourse of the Three Heaps three times by day and three times by night for seven years. On the second ground he should recite it for six years, on the third for five years, on the fourth for four years, on the fifth for three years, on the sixth for two years, and on the seventh for one year. If he sees an image of the Tathāgata made of clay, the bodhisattva should now be regarded as one on the first ground. Whatever is seen on the seven grounds should also be understood as the karma of the seven grounds. On the ninth and tenth grounds there are no false visions in dreams. When he sees himself surrounded by tathāgatas, he should be regarded as one on the tenth ground, unless it is the work of Māra."

The element of space, and likewise the element of beings,
the awakening of the buddhas, and likewise the mind of awakening:
these four things are immeasurable;
the whole world cannot fathom them.
Though someone be ill-favored, dull, or without means,
one to be scorned and held in low regard,
since people's dispositions and conduct are hard to know,
do not rashly impute faults to them.
If not even all the arhats know their own minds,
what need to speak of knowing the conduct of beings?
Ajātaśatru killed his father
and plotted to put the Tathāgata to death;
yet if by a single thought of his he was freed from the lower realms,
what need to speak of a person who is pure?
Just as gold, though thrown into filth,
never loses its shining nature,
so whoever is certain, though engaged in worldly affairs,
never abandons the nature of that intention.
Those who do not forget the mind of awakening
gain what is to be gained and live well.
Like those who seek wealth upon the great ocean,
so do they find their gains complete.
Delighting in the wilderness, ever restraining the senses,
taking up diligence, keeping kin far away,
abiding in loving-kindness, and skilled in the ultimate:
such a one will attain such an awakening.

From the Ratnakoṭi: Mañjuśrī, "buddhahood" is a synonym for the dharmadhātu. The powers, the fearlessnesses, and so on up to the unshared qualities are also to be understood as the dharmadhātu. Within the dharmadhātu, nothing is seen as distinct, from ordinary beings up to the qualities of a buddha. Just as one cannot point to anything distinct and say, "This is the Ganges," or another river, once they have flowed into the ocean, so within the dharmadhātu one cannot point to the qualities of ordinary beings and the rest as distinct.

From the Noble Great Sound: Suppose someone were to fill a four-continent world with gold and other precious things, or fill a great trichiliocosm with buildings like the Vaijayanta palace, and offer it to the Saṅgha of the four directions. Far greater than that is the merit of one who places relics of the Tathāgata the size of a mustard seed inside a stūpa vase the size of a myrobalan, sets up a central pole the size of a needle, and fastens on it a parasol the size of a juniper leaf; or who makes an image of the Tathāgata one span high.

Ajātaśatru killed his father, who had entered the stream. Because he then performed acts of worship and painting for an eighth share of the Tathāgata's relics, he went to hell only once, and by that very root of virtue he will not fall into wrong ways for twenty eons. Having gone forth, he will actualize the awakening of a solitary buddha and will be called Victorious Host.

From Maitreya's Entry: He declared: "Son of good family, only eighty-four thousand eons after the bodhisattva Maitreya had truly entered upon the path did I first generate the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. Maitreya generated the mind of awakening in the presence of the tathāgata called Excellent Gold, when he was the universal monarch called Golden Color."

He declared: "A bodhisattva may have gone against other trainings of the Tathāgata, or committed the faults of the four things that I have said lead to downfall, or remain having taken up the ten paths of nonvirtuous action, or even have committed the five deeds of immediate retribution; yet if he has not abandoned or given up the mind of awakening, is not parted from the mind of awakening, and does not give rise to a mind that takes objects or a mind of clinging, then that bodhisattva is indeed possessed of moral discipline. Why is that? Because these things do not obstruct the bodhisattva's awakening. Even though, having committed evil deeds, he is born into existence, to whatever extent he is born into existence, to that extent he brings the perfections to completion."

If the merit taught to be the ripening of the mind of awakening
were to have a form,
even if it filled realms as many as the grains of sand,
filling them would not use up even a fraction of it,
as with the ocean and a drop of water.
If as many beings as there are in this buddhafield
were each to become a buddha, every one for an eon,
and for many eons did no other work,
though they told of its merit they would never reach its end:
so do they teach the ripening of the mind of awakening.
Someone may fill a trichiliocosm with jewels
and present it to those who possess the ten powers;
one who makes the aspiration for awakening
increases far more merit than that.
Suppose all beings became buddhas
possessed of boundless eloquence,
and that a single being made offerings to them
for eons as long as saṃsāra has been in the past;
when any wise person whatever,
having generated the mind set on supreme awakening,
offers a single flower, this merit
will be far superior to that.
Suppose all beings became arhats
who had attained mastery of wisdom,
and some person who desired merit
made offerings to them for koṭis of niyutas of eons;
when someone, having given rise to compassion,
generates the vast mind set on awakening,
and, having generated the chief of minds, takes seven steps,
this merit will be far superior to that.

It was asked: "Blessed One, why is the bodhisattva Maitreya called Maitreya, the Loving One? From where did he receive this name?" He declared: "When the tathāgata Precious Parasol appeared, a monk called Steadfast Intelligence taught the Dharma to thirty-six hundred thousand koṭis of niyutas of beings in such a way that they would accomplish loving-kindness. Whenever that monk went to a village, he went having entered the samādhi of loving-kindness. As soon as he set foot on the threshold of a village or other place, all the beings there came to abide in loving-kindness and entered the samādhi of loving-kindness. The gods proclaimed, 'Alala! This great being will be the Loving One!' and that tathāgata too rejoiced in the name; therefore he is called Maitreya."

He declared to Gaṅgottarā: "Gaṅgottarā, just as, though one speaks of a 'self', no self whatsoever is found, so too, though I have examined everything from form up to nirvāṇa, ultimately none of it exists and none of it is found."

From the Prophecy of Brahmaśrī:
Thus the Buddha is inconceivable,
and so too are the Buddha's qualities;
for those with faith in the inconceivable,
the ripening likewise is inconceivable.

From the Purification of Karmic Obscurations: He declared: "Mañjuśrī, as for what is called obscuration: desire is an obscuration; aversion and delusion are obscurations; generosity is an obscuration; moral discipline, patience, diligence, meditative concentration and wisdom are obscurations. To perceive in terms of 'buddha' is an obscuration. To perceive in terms of Dharma, Saṅgha, emptiness, signlessness and wishlessness is an obscuration."

It was asked: "How are generosity and the rest, up to wisdom, obscurations?"

He declared: "Although there is no obscuration in any phenomenon, childish ordinary beings, through their own conceptual thought, turn even generosity and the rest into obscurations. Why is that? When childish people practice generosity, they lose faith in beings who are miserly. Through that lack of faith they give rise to anger and the fault of resentment, and through that fault they fall into hell. Likewise, one who guards moral discipline disparages those of broken discipline; through his disparagement many people meet with blame, and because of his lack of faith in them he goes to the lower realms. One who cultivates patience thinks, 'These people harbor the grounds of malice.' One who undertakes diligence thinks, 'These lazy ones are not fit to use even the water of a ditch given in faith.' One who settles in meditative concentration thinks, 'These people are distracted,' and so holds them in contempt. To the extent that they give rise to thoughts of contempt, to that extent they take hold of saṃsāra. Later, when they go on to practice for awakening, although they are learned, they imagine that in all phenomena, which are beyond letters, there is consciousness; and fixing on wisdom as an object, they set out for the far shore. These I call ignorant. Even if they have entered the Great Vehicle, they think, 'We are foremost in the world,' and disparage the followers of the śrāvaka vehicle; through that evil intent and ill speech they will come to go to the lower realms. Mañjuśrī, for one who abides in an illusion-like mind, no phenomenon whatever is an obscuration."

Mañjuśrī said: "To focus on the qualities of a buddha in order to abandon the afflictions is to become impoverished."

He declared: "A bodhisattva who regards all afflictions as the qualities of a buddha thereby incurs a karmic obscuration."

From the Samādhi of the Four Youths:
Whoever, knowing that intrinsic nature is thus empty,
meditates without abiding in any entity,
abandons the whole net of afflictions
and becomes a buddha who has destroyed desire for existence.
Whoever knows emptiness to be empty
and does not think even of emptiness:
those who do not attain emptiness
are the ones who know emptiness.

From the Ten Wheels: He declared: "Thus, suppose someone, for a hundred eons, single-mindedly goes for refuge to great bodhisattvas as many as the sands of the Ganges river, such as Maitreya, Mañjuśrī, Avalokiteśvara and Samantabhadra, recites their names, worships them by focusing on them, and makes aspirations. If, rather than that, someone does the same toward the bodhisattva Kṣitigarbha for a single instant, a mere moment, all his hopes will quickly be fulfilled."

The Essence of true beings,
endowed with many excellent qualities,
bearing and displaying the guise of an ascetic,
arrived and bowed to the great Teacher.

The Tathāgata does not make beings who have become fit vessels turn their backs on the vehicle of gods and humans and head for the lower realms.

He declared: "If a yogin does these ten things of worldly people, not even a root of virtue of the desire realm is accomplished, let alone higher ones; and even what was accomplished before declines and comes to nothing."

What are the ten? Having one's resolve worn down by the requisites of livelihood; corrupt moral discipline; clinging to what is wrong; attachment to worldly omens; not inclining toward the noble ones; an excited mind; slander; causing division; disparaging and exploiting the noble ones; and, through wrong view, denying cause and effect.

Furthermore, if a yogin possesses these ten things, samādhi is not accomplished, and even if it is accomplished, it declines. What are the ten? Delighting in and being attached to things to be done as work; delighting in idle talk; delighting in sleep; delighting in seeking out people; delighting in forms; delighting in sounds; delighting in smells; delighting in tastes; delighting in tangibles; and delighting in conception and analysis. Like Udraka, Arka, Deśakupālika, Devadatta and others, having fallen from meditative concentration one falls into the Avīci hell.

Upāli asked: "You have said, 'Guard the four roots of moral discipline; keep the mind even toward all; do not scorn, do not disparage, do not suddenly bring up faults.' What means, then, should the Saṅgha employ toward those of broken discipline?"

He declared: "I have given non-Buddhists and householders no occasion whatever to accuse a monk of faults. If even the Saṅgha is not permitted to accuse someone without having ascertained the matter and in a way not in accordance with the Dharma, what need is there to speak of expelling him? If one accuses without having ascertained the matter and not in accordance with the Dharma, one incurs the sin of a great wrongdoing. Upāli, if one accuses in these ten ways, without having ascertained the matter and not in accordance with the Dharma, one incurs a great sin. What are they? Accusing before a king who has no faith in the Saṅgha; among wandering ascetics who have no faith; before ministers who have no faith; before merchants, householders, many women, and many who do wrong; before monks and nuns; before those who bear a grudge; and before those who are angry and resentful.

"Even so, that monk is not a vessel for the Dharma. Having fallen from the noble Dharma, he defiles the pure Saṅgha and makes all the activities of an ascetic improper. He is not permitted to make use of the goods of the Saṅgha of the four directions. Nevertheless, because he was fully ordained in a harmonious Saṅgha and has not given up the vow he obtained, he is superior to all householders. If even one who has fallen from the roots is like this, what need is there to speak of one who has fallen in other minor prescribed offenses? Therefore kings, ministers and householders are not permitted to scorn them or to punish them.

"In the future, kṣatriyas, householders and caṇḍālas will harm those who have taken refuge in the teaching and gone forth, even those who have become vessels for the Dharma, doing everything from reviling them up to depriving them of life. They will fall into the great Avīci hell."

Ten oil-pressers' places of sin
equal the sin of one prostitute's place.
Ten prostitutes' places of sin
equal the sin of one liquor-seller's place.
Ten liquor-sellers' places of sin
equal the sin of one butcher's place.
Ten butchers' places of sin
equal the sin of one caṇḍāla king.

Everyone should have faith in and honor the true Dharma of the three vehicles. One should not deride it or denigrate it, nor obstruct or hinder it. If one causes even a single verse of the true Dharma of the three vehicles to disappear, one will without doubt fall into the Avīci hell. If people deride and denigrate one of the three vehicles, one should not live together with them; not even for the least moment should one render them service or listen to the Dharma from them. If one questions such a person and listens to him, by that cause and condition one will fall into the Avīci hell. Why is that? When I was practicing the conduct of a bodhisattva, I attained unsurpassed awakening through hearing, in discussion, even a few verses of all three vehicles.

Abiding in loving-kindness, one abandons aversion and anger. Abiding in compassion, one rescues beings in distress. Abiding in joy, one abandons the four nonvirtuous actions of speech. Abiding in equanimity, one abandons all desire, miserliness and jealousy.

Having faith in the Dharma of the two vehicles, they do not come to have faith in the Great Vehicle. Faith in the Dharma of the Great Vehicle, spoken only in the words of the vehicle, does not achieve its aim.
Inwardly they hold the nihilist view,
yet claim to be followers of the Great Vehicle;
they do not guard against the three kinds of nonvirtuous action,
and they destroy my true Dharma, the wretches.
When such persons die and pass on,
they are sure to fall into the Avīci hell.

Even if a rotting body is smeared with sweet perfume, it does not become fragrant. Likewise, beings who are not diligent in the Dharma of the two vehicles and have no faith in it, and who have not abandoned the ten nonvirtues, cannot realize the profound Dharma of the Great Vehicle. Even if the Great Vehicle is taught to those who deny causes, it does not bring benefit to themselves or to others. Dependent origination itself is emptiness; there is no emptiness apart from dependently originated phenomena.

An evildoer who delights in broken discipline,
having come upon the supreme qualities of the Great Vehicle,
lays claim to the Great Vehicle for the sake of gain and fame,
like a donkey wearing a lion's skin.
Today, here, I declare to the whole assembly:
whoever would swiftly reach this supreme awakening,
apply yourselves to the ten paths of virtuous action;
do not disparage my Dharma, but guard it well.
What I said before in other sūtras,
"Those who desire great awakening, practice the Great Vehicle;
give up the vehicles of śrāvakas and solitary buddhas!",
is Dharma taught to a pure assembly.
Some are endowed with the Dharma of the śrāvaka vehicle;
some delight in the truths and awaken by themselves;
some are fit to delight in supreme wisdom;
therefore teach the three vehicles according to inclination.
Those who seek liberation through the austere practices alone,
if they hear the Great Vehicle explained, fall into the lower realms.

The last of the thousand buddhas of the Fortunate Eon is called Illuminator. If anyone says that one can become fully awakened without properly accomplishing the ten virtuous actions, or says that one who guards not even some of the ten virtuous actions is properly practicing the Great Vehicle, or that he is accomplishing unsurpassed awakening, he speaks falsely. In the presence of the buddhas of the ten directions he deceives the world, shamelessly speaks of emptiness and holds the nihilist view; and so, when he passes from this life, he will be born in the lower realms.

Why is that? Because the ten virtuous actions are the root of the Great Vehicle; they are like the lineage of awakening and the stairway to nirvāṇa.

For example, when incense is burned, although the incense itself is consumed and disappears, its fragrance lets others smell it. Likewise, even a monk of broken moral discipline is not a dead field of merit. Although, consumed by what is given in faith day and night, he goes to the lower realms, he becomes a source of benefit and happiness for countless beings and lets them smell the fragrance of the higher realms and of nirvāṇa. Thus, although that monk is not a vessel for the Dharma, because he has shaved his hair and beard and wears saffron robes, his appearance and deportment accord with those of the noble ones, and merely by seeing him countless beings will increase and give rise to roots of virtue of many kinds. Therefore kings and others have no right to punish any of them.

From the Advice to the King:
Give up wealth to protect the body;
give up a limb to protect life;
wealth and limbs, and likewise life,
give up them all to protect the Dharma here.
The Dharma ever protects the one who practices the Dharma,
as a great parasol shelters one in the rainy season.
This is the benefit of practicing the Dharma:
one who practices the Dharma does not go to the lower realms.
One who practices what is not Dharma and is heedless,
dying and passing on from here, goes to the unbearable lower realms.
A place where one could stay and not be overcome by death,
such a place does not exist:
not in the sky, not within the ocean;
even entering a mountain cave is of no help.
Once nirvāṇa has been attained, from then on one does not die.

From the Full Embrace of the Roots of Virtue: "What is called 'letterless' is the inexpressible, and letters are nothing but expression."

He declared: "Ajita, it is like this. Just as, without butter, the stream of the essence of butter is cut off, so, without the generation of the mind of awakening, the Tathāgata does not come into being. Whatever is elaborated, that is awakening. Why is that? Because awakening is free of elaboration; this is the principle. Wherever there is elaboration, to that same extent there is awakening. Why is that? Because all elaborations are void, and that awakening too is void."

Having overcome the two extremes,
one engages the elements by the Middle Way.
Therefore acceptance should be taught:
that phenomena are empty of essential nature.
That is the path of awakening,
and that is what I too have taught.

What is called right view is where there is no view at all. All views are wrong views.

"Suppose someone were to deprive of life the arhats who fill a great trichiliocosm world-system. I say that if someone turns a bodhisattva's inclination away from the mind of awakening, the heap of demerit is far greater than that. Śāriputra, in short, for one who sets his mind on obstructing the generation of the mind set on awakening, there is no going to nirvāṇa and no perfect endowment of freedom."

One who possesses four qualities should be recognized as a spiritual friend. What are they? He has words of rebuke; he has the profound Dharma; he directs one to the profound Dharma; from time to time he has words of instruction; and from time to time he leaves one alone.

In order to make the superknowledges swift, one should devote oneself to worshiping, repairing and cleaning the stūpas of the Tathāgata. Whoever cleans them attains the supreme aspiration for beauty of form, the perfect endowment of freedom, a steadfast commitment, and the supreme aspiration to behold the Tathāgata.

From the Chapter on Mañjuśrī's Miraculous Display: For a yogin, to rely on and serve those who wish only for their own liberation and are terrified of saṃsāra is the work of Māra. To conceptualize emptiness and abandon sentient beings is the work of Māra. To conceptualize the unconditioned and grow weary of conditioned roots of virtue is also the work of Māra. To seek the exhaustion of all afflictions without remainder and, within the stream of saṃsāra, to disparage the afflictions is also the work of Māra.

When a monk cultivates the view of the perishing collection as the action of the single path in its essential nature, knowing nondually and as equal that whatever is the essential nature of the view of the perishing collection is the essential nature of the action of the single path; and when, seeking what arises from ignorance, existence and craving, or investigating one by one what arises from knowledge, craving and existence, he knows that the essential nature of knowledge, existence and craving is itself knowledge and liberation, then he is free of overweening pride.

By four qualities one attains miraculous powers: lightness of body, lightness of mind, not clinging to any phenomenon, and blessing the four elements as equal to the element of space.

By four qualities one comes to be born miraculously: having images of the Tathāgata made seated upon lotuses; filling one's hands with lotuses and the like and scattering them over images and stūpas; a disposition that brings many benefits and gives joy, and not seizing on the faults of others; and dedicating all roots of virtue to buddhahood for the sake of sentient beings.

By four qualities one comes to have great wisdom: not being miserly with the Dharma; dispelling the regret of those who have acted wrongly; giving instruction exactly as it truly is to those who request it; and dwelling much in the cultivation of emptiness.

By four qualities one attains meeting with the buddhas: not abandoning the true Dharma even for the sake of one's life; not relying on evil friends even for the sake of one's life; not denigrating those who teach the Dharma even for the sake of one's life; and cultivating the samādhi of recollecting the Buddha.

By four qualities one attains the thirty-two marks of a great being: scattering handfuls of jewels over the stūpas of the Tathāgata; washing the stūpas of the Tathāgata with fragrant sesame oil; worshiping stūpas with incense, flowers, cymbals and the like; and honoring one's preceptor and master by serving them with pleasing attendance.

By four qualities one attains the eighty excellent minor marks: offering seats made of all kinds of materials; never tiring of showing respect to others; not starting quarrels with those who teach the Dharma; and causing beings to take up awakening properly.

By four qualities one is born in whatever buddhafield one has set one's mind on: not being jealous of the profit and gain that others come by; seeking with superior intention the complete purity of the six perfections; giving rise to the perception of all bodhisattvas as the Teacher; and not putting on a false show for the sake of profit.

From the Teaching on Conventional and Ultimate Truth: With the flesh eye one sees all beings and the buddhafields. With the divine eye one sees the death, passing on and birth of beings. With the wisdom eye one knows the workings and movements of beings' minds. With the Dharma eye one sees beings exactly as they truly are. With the Buddha eye one performs all the deeds of a buddha.

Relying on a nonvirtuous friend is the door to all that is nonvirtuous. Relying on a spiritual friend is the door to all roots of virtue. The mind of awakening is the door to all the qualities of a buddha.

From the Samādhi of the Seal of the Tathāgata's Wisdom: He declared: "Maitreya, of these three thousand koṭis of bodhisattvas, eight thousand will remain upholding and preserving the true Dharma. The remaining bodhisattvas will become unruly, attached to grasping at objects, and not upholders of the true Dharma. In the last five hundred years of the later time, they will reject this awakening of mine, which I accomplished over many countless eons; they will oppose it, and they will uphold what was not taught.

"Why is that? Maitreya, the mind set on awakening is generated in seven ways. What are the seven? A bodhisattva generates the mind set on awakening through the buddhas, the blessed ones. At the time when the true Dharma is being destroyed, a bodhisattva generates the mind set on awakening in order to preserve the true Dharma. Seeing the realm of beings tormented by every kind of suffering, great compassion arises, and a bodhisattva generates the mind set on awakening. Having shown honor and given fine and pleasing gifts, a bodhisattva generates the mind set on awakening himself. Seeing others generate the mind set on awakening, a bodhisattva generates the mind set on awakening. Having heard the full praise of the qualities of the Tathāgata's body, with its many adornments of the major and minor marks, a bodhisattva generates the mind set on awakening. The first three protect the awakening of the buddhas, and one swiftly becomes irreversible from unsurpassed awakening. The remaining four become unruly bodhisattvas who are not upholders of the true Dharma.

"If a bodhisattva possesses five qualities, he should be known to be of an irreversible nature. The five are: an even mind toward all beings; no jealousy of the gain and honor of others; not speaking ill of a monk who upholds the true Dharma, even for the sake of his life; no attachment to profit, gain, honor or fame; and devotion to the profound Dharma.

"If he possesses five qualities, a bodhisattva is unruly. The five are: he has little splendor and is hard to admonish; he is devoted to the inferior and teaches inferior Dharma to everyone; attached to profit, gain and honor, he is possessive of the households that give him alms; he is full of deceit and guile; and, devoting himself to talk of emptiness, his conduct does not match it.

"Maitreya, in later times, in the age to come, those bodhisattvas who do not lose heart, who display their commitment, and who have entered upon awakening from the depths of their hearts, have served many countless buddhas. Whatever karma they have formed that would lead to the lower realms will be purified merely by their becoming ugly. Their karmic obscurations will be cleansed merely by having many illnesses, by being born into a low family, by being poor, by being born in a borderland or in a family of wrong views, by having much sorrow on account of beings, by not meeting a spiritual friend, by not being able to hear the Dharma continually, by having a distracted mind in meditation, and even by seeing evil things in a dream."

Whatever mind thinks "empty" is not right,
for by it the unborn is imagined.
All conceptual thoughts are Māra's entry;
this is called the seal of the changeless Dharma.
If the unborn is the one truth,
some nonetheless speak of four truths.
Seated at the heart of awakening, I did not see
even one truth established; how then could there be four?

From the Illusion-like Samādhi: After the tathāgata Amitābha has passed into nirvāṇa, the true Dharma will remain for eighty-four hundred thousand koṭis of niyutas of eons. At dawn on the very night that the true Dharma disappears, Avalokiteśvara, the Lord, will become fully awakened and will be called King of the Glorious Peak Exalted by Light Rays on Every Side. That world-system will be called Gathering of All Jewels. After him the bodhisattva Mahāsthāmaprāpta will become awakened there and will be called King of the Heap of Jewels of Well-Established Qualities. Any woman who hears this Dharma discourse will at once be freed from the state of womanhood, except where the two causes, miserliness and jealousy, remain.

From the Samādhi of the Miracle of Utter Peace and Certainty: If a bodhisattva generates a mind endowed with compassion, then by that root of virtue, even if he is born in Jambudvīpa when no buddha has appeared, he will become a true being and will be watched over by the gods. Whenever he becomes heedless, he will be urged on again by the sons of the gods of the Pure Abodes, and his mindfulness will not be lost.

He also fully upholds five pure things: pure moral discipline, a pure body, the purity of the perfect marks, the purity of cultivated roots of virtue, and the purity of reliance on the Three Jewels, on bodhisattvas and on the mind of awakening.

He declared: "Compare someone who takes the lives of all the beings of Jambudvīpa and robs them of everything they own with someone who obstructs a bodhisattva's root of virtue, even the giving of a single morsel of food to an animal: the sin of the latter is incalculably greater. Not seeing any phenomenon at all is seeing the Tathāgata."
If one generates the mind set on awakening,
then even through koṭis of eons
one will never become blind or lame,
and one will become the unsurpassed Teacher.

He declared: "Mañjuśrī, I have not seen a single thing so greatly blameworthy as a bodhisattva's giving rise to a single thought of anger toward a bodhisattva. Mañjuśrī, for as many eons as the thoughts of anger or contempt that a bodhisattva gives rise to toward a bodhisattva, he must put on the armor to dwell in the hells. Mañjuśrī, apart from the denigration of a bodhisattva, nothing other than a bodhisattva can bring about a wrongful downfall."

From the Questions of Ratnacandra: Ratnacandra, the son of King Bimbisāra, asked, "Blessed One, please explain the names of the ten buddhas of the ten directions such that whoever holds them in mind becomes irreversible from unsurpassed awakening," and so on. In reply he declared: "In the east, in the world-system Even as the Palm of the Hand, dwells the buddha Aśoka. In the south, in the world-system Joyous, dwells the buddha Candanaśrī. In the west, in the world-system Excellent, dwells the buddha Infinite Light. In the north, in the world-system Unshakable, dwells the buddha Ketuśrī. In the southeast, in the world-system Moonlight, dwells the buddha Aśokaśrī. In the southwest, in the world-system Bannered, dwells the buddha Jewel Lampstand. In the northwest, in the world-system Resounding, dwells the buddha Puṣpaśrī. In the northeast, in the world-system Blissful, dwells the buddha Who Sports in the Lesser and Greater Superknowledges. Below, in the world-system Spacious, dwells the buddha Glory of Light. Above, in the world-system Moonlike, dwells the buddha Glory of Wealth."

From the Dhāraṇī of the Jewel Torch:
In people who have no faith
the bright qualities do not grow,
just as no green shoot springs
from a seed that fire has burned.

From the Gaṇḍavyūha: The mind of awakening is like the seed of all the qualities of a buddha.

From the Teaching of Vimalakīrti: Even when one has given rise to a view of the perishing collection as great as Sumeru, the mind of awakening arises, and from it the qualities of a buddha arise.

From the Ratnakūṭa: A bodhisattva, though he dwells within the shell of ignorance, has not broken through the view of self and has not emerged from the three realms, yet utters the sound of the three doors of liberation as the very voice of a buddha.

Since the Śūraṅgama Sūtra says that even generating the mind of awakening deceitfully is a cause of buddhahood, what need is there to speak of generating the mind after doing a few virtuous deeds?

In the Bhadrakalpika Sūtra: The tathāgata King of Stars, when he was a cowherd, offered a leaf for cleaning the teeth and first generated the mind set on awakening. The tathāgata Renowned, when he was a weaver, offered the fringe of a cloth; Radiant, when he was a village beggar, offered a lamp of grass; and Hard to Tame, when he was a gatherer of firewood, offered a tooth-stick; and so they first generated the mind set on awakening.

Even when it is not matched by conduct, the mind of awakening produces boundless happiness. How is this shown? From Maitreya's Liberation: "Son of good family, just as a precious vajra, even when broken, outshines all ornaments of gold, does not lose the name 'precious vajra', and dispels all poverty, so too the precious vajra of generating the mind set on omniscience, even when it lacks diligent practice, outshines all the qualities of śrāvakas and solitary buddhas, does not lose the name 'bodhisattva', and dispels all the poverty of saṃsāra."

From the Gaṇḍavyūha Sūtra, as taught by the noble Śrīsambhava: "Son of good family, bodhisattvas who are well cared for by spiritual friends do not fall into the lower realms. They do not go against the trainings of a bodhisattva. They carry out all their conduct without forgetfulness. They are hard for karma and the afflictions to overcome. The spiritual friend turns one back from the state of heedlessness and drives one out of the city of saṃsāra. Therefore, son of good family, you should go to spiritual friends with unbroken attention. Serve the spiritual friend with a mind like the earth, never wearied by any burden; like a vajra, being indivisible; like a servant, taking on every task; like a sweeper, with pride subdued; like a dog, being without anger; and like a wise son, looking to the face of the spiritual friend. Son of good family, you should regard yourself as a patient, the spiritual friend as a physician, the instructions as medicine, and diligent practice as the cure of the illness."

From the Questions of Siṃha:
"That in every birth the mind of awakening,
that this mind, even in dreams,
should not be given up, let alone when awake:
by what does this come about?"
He declared:
"In villages and in towns,
or in whatever lands one dwells,
establish others properly in awakening;
by this the mind will not be given up."

From the Heap of Jewels: Cloth that has been offered to a stūpa may be left to wear away at that very stūpa through wind, sun and rain; but cloth goods offered to a stūpa may not be turned into gold, for a stūpa is in no way in want of the stūpa's goods.

From the Śūraṅgama Samādhi: Know that if one person takes the measure of another, he will be harmed.

From the Sūtra Comprising All Dharmas in Full Extent: Whoever is disrespectful to one who teaches the Dharma does not wish to see the Buddha.

Whoever speaks harshly to one who teaches the Dharma completely abandons the Buddha.

"Maitreya, concerning the six perfections that bodhisattvas properly accomplish for the sake of awakening, those deluded people say this: 'A bodhisattva should properly accomplish the perfection of wisdom; what use are the remaining perfections in which he is to train?' If they think to discredit the other perfections, they should consider this: When I was the king of Kāśi and gave my flesh to a hawk for the sake of a dove, was that a sign of corrupt wisdom?"

He replied: "It was not."

"When I was practicing the conduct of awakening, did the roots of virtue that I accomplished, endowed with the six perfections, do me harm?"

"They did not."

"If those deluded people say, 'Awakening is solely the one way of emptiness,' their conduct is not completely pure."

From the Ratnakūṭa Sūtra: By four things the roots of virtue that have arisen in a bodhisattva are exhausted: seeking out the secret sayings of the Lokāyata out of pride; frequenting householders out of attachment to learning and honor; being angry at bodhisattvas and denigrating them; and rejecting sūtras one has not grasped or heard.

From the scripture of the Sarvāstivāda: He declared: "Look at this monk who, with a clear and faithful mind, has prostrated to the stūpa."
"Yes, venerable one."
"As many grains of sand as there are in the earth his body covered, down to eighty-four thousand yojanas below, so many times over will this monk enjoy the kingdom of a universal monarch a thousandfold."
The venerable Upāli asked: "Where would such great roots of virtue be used up?"
He declared: "I see nothing like a fellow in the holy life wounding or injuring a fellow in the holy life; there these great roots of virtue are used up entirely. Therefore one should not harm even a stump with one's mind, let alone a body that has a mind."

From the Sūtra of Bhaiṣajyaguru, Lapis Light: Those who, having held the four hundred training points of a bodhisattva, the two hundred and fifty training points of a monk's vow, or the five hundred of a nun, have fallen away from them and fear the lower realms, will be freed from the lower realms if they make all kinds of offerings to Bhaiṣajyaguru.

From the Noble Liberation of Maitreya: Just as a single lamp, as soon as it is brought into a house or a room, dispels even darkness that has gathered for a thousand years, so too, as soon as a single thought set on omniscience is generated, it dispels the karma and afflictions accumulated over inexpressible eons.

From the Moon Lamp Sūtra:
Zeal for the good of beings, the mind of awakening,
whatever mind is secluded through proper attention,
and constant heedfulness are the cause of the deathless;
not grasping is the cause of every happiness.

From the Kāśyapa Chapter: By four things a bodhisattva's wisdom degenerates. The four are: disrespect for the Dharma and for those who teach the Dharma; being miserly with the Dharma and keeping a teacher's closed fist; obstructing the Dharma for a person who longs for it, turning back his aspiration and not teaching him the Dharma; and, out of pride, praising oneself and disparaging others.

By four things the mind of awakening is forgotten. The four are: deceiving one's teacher, one's guru and those worthy of offerings; causing regret in others where there was none; speaking ill of persons who have entered the Great Vehicle; and dealing with others through deceit and guile, not with superior intention.

By four things the mind of awakening is not forgotten until one reaches the heart of awakening, and becomes manifest as soon as one is born. The four are: not lying even for the sake of one's life, not even in jest; abiding with superior intention toward all beings, free of deceit and guile; regarding all bodhisattvas as the Teacher and proclaiming their praises in the four directions; and, because one does not wish for a limited vehicle, ripening beings and causing them to take up unsurpassed awakening.

These four are the true qualities of a bodhisattva. What are they?
Though he always holds phenomena to be empty,
he also has faith in the ripening of karma.
Though through acceptance of selflessness he abides in equality,
he gives rise to great compassion for beings.
His intention always abides in nirvāṇa;
his practice abides in saṃsāra.
Though he gives gifts in order to ripen beings,
he has no expectation of the ripening of karma.

Poison held in check by mantras and medicines cannot kill. Likewise, the poison of the afflictions of a bodhisattva held in check by means and wisdom cannot make him fall into wrong ways.
Just as the filth and refuse of cities
benefits fields of sugarcane,
so the refuse of a bodhisattva's afflictions
benefits the Dharma of the Victor.

Emptiness is the way out from all views. Kāśyapa, I have declared that one who views emptiness as a thing in itself is incurable.

A bodhisattva who has first generated the mind of awakening dwells within the shell of the egg of ignorance, his eyes covered by the darkness and the cataracts of ignorance; even so, with the sound of his voice dedicating his roots of virtue, he outshines all śrāvakas and solitary buddhas.

If one asks how that moves which not even all the buddhas have seen, except as something arising from perception engaged in error: Kāśyapa, the mind is like an illusion.

What are the four lower realms? The hells, the birthplaces of animals, the world of Yama, and the asuras.

Devotion to freedom from strife is the way of an ascetic.

Whoever serves one who teaches the Dharma of the Tathāgata will see the Tathāgata at the time of death, and will have the ten bodily blessings: he does not die in the grip of painful feelings; his eyes do not roll in confusion; he does not flail his hands; he does not thrash his feet; he does not void urine; no excrement comes out; no dust rises at his heart; he does not clench his fists; he does not clutch at the air; and just as he is sitting, so he lets go of the formations of life.

From Cutting the Stream of Karmic Obscuration: I have declared that among all gifts the gift of the Dharma is supreme.

The seven precious things are gold, silver, pearl, lapis lazuli, crystal, musāragalva, and red pearl.

From the Compendium of All Refutations: When persons who are bodhisattvas who have first generated the mind give rise to thoughts of anger, they completely abandon the Buddha. For as many thoughts of anger as they give rise to, for that many eons they must put on armor in hell. I have not seen any other thing like giving rise to malice toward bodhisattvas, which makes a bodhisattva fall wrongly and go to the lower realms.

"Ajita, this practice of the six perfections by bodhisattvas is for the sake of awakening. Yet foolish people say, 'A bodhisattva should train in the perfection of wisdom alone,' and they discredit the remaining perfections. What do you think of this: when the king of Kāśi gave away his head, was he one of corrupt wisdom?"
He replied: "He was not."

If one regards some of the Tathāgata's words as good and others as bad, that is rejecting the Dharma. If one says, "This was spoken for bodhisattvas, this for śrāvakas, and this for solitary buddhas," that is rejecting the Dharma. Although I made the aspiration that all the eloquence of śrāvakas and bodhisattvas should arise from the power of the Tathāgata, those foolish people, blessed by Māra so that they go to the lower realms, think to find fault with it. They think to find fault with those who teach the Dharma with the Tathāgata's permission. If, without having taken up or read this Dharma discourse, one rejects it, one becomes deprived of the Dharma, and liberation from that is very hard. One who possesses four qualities will not reject it: having no anger toward beings, not opposing those who teach the Dharma, not rejecting others, and respect for all that the Tathāgata has spoken.

From the Great Cloud: "Great brahmin, ask forgiveness of the bodhisattva, the great being Great Yellow, surrounded by his retinue of bodhisattvas. Those who do not know the qualities of Devadatta should be known to have gained no more than half of a buddha's appearance in the world. Great brahmin, look at the greatness of Devadatta's qualities. Regard him as a great being, like the domain of the Tathāgata: wherever the Tathāgata appears for the sake of those to be trained, there this great being too appears, following after him. Rejoice in him, surrounded by the group of six bodhisattvas. Bodhisattvas always play in the hells by means of their superknowledges; this is the domain of bodhisattvas. Devadatta is not one who goes to hell."

"Goddess, when one hundred and twenty years have passed after the death of the master, the prophesying brahmin Kauṇḍinya, he will be born into the line of universal monarchs called the Maurya clan, and once born he will become a universal monarch over half the world."
She asked: "What will his name be?"
"Goddess, he will be called the lay king Aśoka."

The Buddha does not pass into final nirvāṇa,
nor does the Dharma ever decline;
it is in order to ripen beings
that he displays the final nirvāṇa.

This Great Cloud too will circulate in Jambudvīpa for forty years, and it will also spread widely. At the time when the true Dharma has utterly declined, one will be born in Jambudvīpa as a monk like Mahākāśyapa, and he will speak in praise of the established bases of training.

Many sons of the gods asked: "How long will it be until a monk like Mahākāśyapa appears? Where will he be born? We wish to know what his name will be."

He declared: "When many hundreds of years have passed after my passing, in the southern region there will arise a king of the citadel called Blissful Conduct. At that time, when he is eighty years old, when the time has come that only a remnant of the true Dharma is left before it disappears, my śrāvaka will appear. He will uphold the Dharma and will also teach the Great Vehicle extensively to others. For that monk made the aspiration before the tathāgata Lamp of the Nāga Lineage to uphold the teaching of Śākyamuni. That ship of great diligence is this very Licchavi, Sarvalokapriyadarśana. Know that he will become the monk unlike the Tathāgata. Look further at the greatness of my śrāvaka, the monk unlike the Tathāgata, who will thrice proclaim the great sound of the Dharma and then die. At that time the body of my true Dharma will disappear. Passing from here at death, he will become a buddha. After this Fortunate Eon has passed, for sixty-two eons no buddha will appear, but six hundred thousand koṭis of solitary buddhas will appear. When those eons have passed, seven buddhas will appear. When the seven have passed into final nirvāṇa, this world-system will be called Manifestly Embraced, and there he will become the tathāgata called Light of the Source of Wisdom.

"Those great bodhisattvas who rightly see the perceptions of reality, permanence, self and being, I call those of perfectly right view."

It was asked: "With what in mind did you fully explain the false perceptions of the world, and speak as you did?"

"What is called perceiving reality, permanence, self and being is a synonym for liberation. 'The Tathāgata is permanent, everlasting and stable' is a synonym for the true Dharma not declining. Beings of little merit give rise to the perception that liberation is extinction, and they think that the Tathāgata, though he remains, has passed into final nirvāṇa. Those who wish to see rightly the supreme eminence of the tathāgatas should give rise, toward a bodhisattva who abides in this samādhi, to the perception of permanence, self and being, and should give rise to the perception of liberation."

This is the account of the prophecy from the hundred-thousand-line chapter of the Great Cloud Sūtra, composed in lines of eight syllables, which foretells as many as koṭis of future beings.

From the Purification of Infinite Gateways: What is the Dharma discourse? Having realized that all conditioned phenomena arise from not knowing, and that all unconditioned phenomena arise from seeing with wisdom, one should rightly engage and fully purify conditioned and unconditioned phenomena with unelaborated wisdom.

Even if people realize the Tathāgata as the nature of reality, their seeing of the Tathāgata is not pure. Why is that? The Tathāgata is neither a phenomenon nor a non-phenomenon. All phenomena are mind only, and are not associated with mind. It is said: "Thinking, 'It would not do for beings who have not practiced the profound Dharma to be confused,' I teach in figurative terms that the Tathāgata, once passed away, no longer exists." A phenomenon that has neither arising nor ceasing cannot be called "dead and gone" or "nonexistent". The Tathāgata too has neither arising nor ceasing.

"All phenomena are like space": how are all phenomena like space? Just as space is not included anywhere, so all phenomena are not included anywhere.

Through the wisdom of omniscience, which is nonconceptual, unattached and unobstructed, the Tathāgata knows fully and exactly as they are all the many kinds of conduct of all beings without exception.

The eldest son of Kubera,
the yakṣa called Saṃjñeya,
dwelling free of strife and anger,
always, together with his father,
will protect whoever
delights in these teachings.

From the Questions of the God's Son Suvikrāntacinta: A bodhisattva who possesses four qualities is free of the darkness that obscures wisdom. The four are: he is never sated with learning; he applies himself thoroughly to what is proper; he teaches the Dharma with a mind free of material concerns; and, in order to know the dharmadhātu as indivisible, he realizes all afflictions to be like illusions. By entering one way he enters the entry of all ways.

Those who possess four qualities are investigators of the ways of the world. The four are: teaching the Dharma to those who engage in idle talk; keeping the Dharma in mind where great crowds gather; rejoicing in great generosity and dedicating it to awakening; and inclining toward solitude, away from all bustle.

One who possesses four qualities speaks as the Buddha has spoken. The four are: not giving up the mind of awakening; making sublime learning the very essence; doing as one says; and entering into selflessness by nature.

One who possesses four qualities is skilled in the causes of all activities. The four are: not giving up generosity, the cause of great wealth; not giving up moral discipline, the cause of the higher realms; not giving up learning, the cause of great wisdom; and not giving up meditation, the cause of freedom.

When a bodhisattva knows that all phenomena are by nature in meditative equipoise, gives rise to great compassion for those whose minds are distracted, and, though he acts until the very end of the future, does not stir from abiding in meditative concentration, to that extent a bodhisattva is a meditator.

When a bodhisattva sees all objects as the domain of the Buddha, and sees that even the domain of the Buddha does not exist, to that extent a bodhisattva guards his senses.

When a bodhisattva listens to all that the Buddha has spoken, realizes the meaning of what he has heard, retains it as he heard it, and teaches it to others as he retained it, he is learned.

Mañjuśrī said: "A bodhisattva's fear arises from the conditioned and also from the unconditioned. From the conditioned arises the fear of the afflictions; from the unconditioned arises the fear of having no afflictions."

"Mañjuśrī, what fear is there in having no afflictions?"

"Divine one, in the three realms there is the fear of the afflictions; if a bodhisattva there does not engage in the afflictions, he becomes a śrāvaka. When a bodhisattva accomplishes the perfection of generosity by not giving up anything completely, moral discipline by passing beyond moral discipline, patience by malice, diligence by laziness, meditative concentration by forgetfulness, and the perfection of wisdom by being inert matter and, from the first thought on, mute as a sheep, to that extent he abides on the path."

"Mañjuśrī, with what in mind do you say this?"

"Son of the gods, these four are things that bodhisattvas never give up: beings, the Dharma, the qualities of awakening, and virtuous qualities. By never giving up these four, they accomplish the perfection of generosity. 'Passing beyond moral discipline' means having gone beyond moral discipline: beyond it to samādhi, beyond that to wisdom, and beyond that to liberation. In this way moral discipline is accomplished. 'Engaging in malice' means being involved with the vehicles of the śrāvakas and solitary buddhas and yet entering the Great Vehicle; and entering the Great Vehicle is the perfection of patience. 'Laziness' means not letting body, speech and mind grow dull; striving in the Dharma in this way is diligence."

"'Forgetfulness' means that even in dreams one has no recollection of the śrāvakas and solitary buddhas; not falling to such a level is itself meditative equipoise in the Great Vehicle, and so it is meditative concentration. 'Inert matter' means that all phenomena are inert; knowing their root and the rest, and their abiding nature, just as they are, is wisdom."

When the mind is free of attachment,
that is the mind of unsurpassed awakening;
direct perception of the nature of mind,
that is the defining mark of wisdom.
In every generation of the mind,
to dedicate it fully to awakening,
and to examine it closely from the mind of awakening,
that is the defining mark of means.
By wisdom that lacks means,
what has not arisen does not arise;
by means that lacks wisdom,
the wise do not reach perfect peace.

The four hundred and four diseases are these: one hundred and one arising from wind, one hundred and one arising from bile, one hundred and one arising from phlegm, and one hundred and one arising from their combination.

Karma is of three kinds: that connected with this life, that connected with the intermediate state, and that connected with another life.

If a man has intercourse with a man, when he parts from his body he will be born in the hell called Dreadful. If one has intercourse with a ewe or a she-donkey, one will be born in the hell called Crying Atsa. If one has intercourse with one's sister or with another unsuitable partner, one will be born in the hell called Crying Alas. If one has intercourse with a nun whose discipline is broken and whose conduct is bad, one will be born in a hell like Unceasing Death. When two parties are in dispute, if a king's minister, or someone else placed in a position of trust, takes money and, out of friendship or under the sway of desire and the like, decides the case crookedly, he will be born in the hell of Eleven Tongues. If one has promised to give goods to those who are suffering, sick, hungry, without protector or destitute, and does not give them, or has promised them food and drink and does not give it, one will be born in the hell called No Hope of Any Joy.

Delighting in what is meaningless,
women are full of guile and jealousy;
ruinous to both worlds,
they stand before men.
Their words are utterly unstable;
they are ever skilled at arousing desire.
Their hearts are set upon another,
while they put on a show in another manner.
At first they are soft and gentle,
but afterwards their minds are like vajra.
Their minds are fickle and of no help;
the honors shown them they forget.
A hundred kindnesses they let go to waste,
a single wrong they remember.
Women are the ground of sin;
their hearts are like the nature of lightning.
If a man has a mind of attachment,
women are a cause of fear.
In this world and the world beyond,
among all things that terrify, women are fearsome.
If you desire supreme happiness,
then give up women in this way.
One who always gives up women
is happy in this world and the next.
Whoever wishes to be rid of poverty,
whoever wishes to prosper,
whoever wishes to go to nirvāṇa,
that man gives up women.
It is as the brahmins say: if one does not take as a bride a young woman whose desire has awakened, it is like taking a human life.

If one has committed all five deeds of immediate retribution, one's body becomes five yojanas in size, and so on down to one yojana if one has committed one. Of those bound for Avīci, for some the fire of Avīci arises from their own bodies at the time of death; others, having died, are born in the intermediate state, and after that come into the Avīci hell.

If one has taken one, two, three, four, five or more bases of training, and seduces daughters or sisters, or women who are like daughters or sisters, or the wives of friends, who are unsuitable, one will be born in the hell called Alala.

The classes of hungry ghosts, in brief, number thirty-six. They are: the crooked-bellied; those with mouths as narrow as the eye of a needle; eaters of excrement; eaters of smells; eaters of incense; drinkers of water; eaters of spittle; eaters of garlands; eaters of flesh; eaters of blood; eaters of smoke; those of violent conduct; those who watch for an opening; the mighty; those who watch for an opening against humans; those whose bodies blaze at night; those who take whatever form they wish; the staffs of Yama; brahmarākṣasas; eaters of filth; the host of Māra; and so on.

If, in a wilderness where water is scarce, one cuts off the water on which creatures depend and by which they live, one is born in the hell where beings cry out "Byajavabha".

If, seeking faults, one listens to the Dharma from a learned person and then does not enter that Dharma and Vinaya but discredits it with ugly words, one is born in the hell called Burning by Fire.

Malice that is held in the heart
no one at all can guard against:
not friends, companions or kin,
nor any wealth of Dharma or riches.
Freedom from strife is inexhaustible wealth,
greater than being lord of all people.
Truth is the ladder to the higher realms;
it alone is the gateway to nirvāṇa.

One who possesses the miraculous power of inclination travels just as he inclines. If he inclines toward the earth element, he sees even space as the earth element and travels across it.

Those who buy when prices are low and then do harm by selling dear, and who are ruined by jealousy and miserliness, are born as hungry ghosts, as brahmarākṣasas, who roam about in the form of brahmins.

The imprints of virtue bring joy
to those long crushed in the lower realms,
just as, though the flower has withered,
its fragrance lingers in the sesame.

Because of the evil deeds they have done, they see even the ocean dried up everywhere, and the mountains and forests, together with the oceans, all ablaze.

From the Noble Sūtra of the Questions of the Nāga King Sāgara: The universal monarch God-Born, having fallen asleep, heard two verses in a dream. When that night had passed, he went with his retinue into the presence of the Blessed One. Having arrived, he generated the mind set on awakening in the presence of the Blessed One and spoke these verses:
Craving forms and relying on sounds,
dwelling on smells, tastes and touch,
craving pleasures and delighting in comfort,
one cannot realize this supreme awakening.

Also from the same source:
In my dream I heard of the mind of awakening;
I have set out for supreme awakening in all things.

From the Teaching of the Tathāgata's Secrets: Śāntamati, furthermore, the speech of the Tathāgata fills all the world-systems of the ten directions far and wide and fully satisfies the intentions of all beings; and yet, Śāntamati, the Tathāgata does not think, "I am clearly explaining and teaching the sūtra collection and the rest; I am proclaiming it in this way." Even so, the assembly gathered around the Tathāgata, the monks and the others, each according to the level of their faculties, receive just that gift of the Dharma by hearing which they will come to understand; and they know that it comes from the Tathāgata's mouth. Yet in that teaching of the Dharma, without any conflict among its distinct and definite words, all of them fully understand each their own teaching. Śāntamati, this is the secret of the Tathāgata's speech.

Also from the same source: The Tathāgata is perfectly in meditative equipoise, and does not in any way at all cognize the conceptual forms of letters; yet beings devoted to letters think, "The Tathāgata is teaching us the Dharma; we understand the Dharma the Tathāgata is teaching." Śāntamati, no sound arises from the Tathāgata's teeth, lips, palate, tongue or mouth, and yet its arising is heard.

Śāntamati, furthermore, the body of the Tathāgata is not divided into aggregates, not divided into elements, not divided into sense sources. It does not arise from karma, it does not arise from the afflictions, it does not arise from father and mother, it does not arise from an embryo of the great elements. It is not covered with skin over flesh and blood; it is not bound together with bones and a network of sinews; no breath moves in and out of it; it is not connected with a life force. Śāntamati, the body of the Tathāgata is a body that is no body: it is the Dharma body, the body without form and without signs. Although it is free of elaboration and of all movement, it appears to the eyes of beings who give priority to form and signs, beings who are to be trained by the display of a body. To beings who give priority to the defining mark of phenomena, free of form, it does not appear to be seen anywhere at all, even with the divine eye.

From the Vinayavastu: In the time of the buddha Kāśyapa there arose King Kṛkin, and there the Buddha too made his aspiration and was born in Tuṣita. From the line of Kṛkin there were one hundred and one generations of kings in the city of Potala. The last of these was a king called Karṇa. He had two sons, called Gautama and Bharadvāja.
